Fuck all cops.
****** Emacs Configuration:
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: 20190617-emacs-configuration
:END:
One kind of perpetual project I have going on is the import of various old projects into whatever my new method of organization is. Well, seems like now is as good a time as any to look through my /Emacs configuration/ and see what I want to keep, and what I want to pull. The first two lines are definitely keepers: they set up the /Emacs/ package management.

#+name: package-initialize-declaration
#+begin_src elisp
  (package-initialize)
  (require 'package)
#+end_src

This next chunk sets up some additional package sources, remote and local, also necessary.

#+name: package-source-declarations
#+begin_src elisp
  (add-to-list 'package-archives
               '("melpa" . "http://melpa.org/packages/") t)
  (add-to-list 'package-archives '("org" . "https://orgmode.org/elpa/") t)
  (eval-when-compile
    (add-to-list 'load-path "~/.emacs.d/elpa")
    (add-to-list 'load-path "~/.emacs.d/git")
    (require 'use-package))
#+end_src

This sets up the /auto-package-update/ package, which automatically updates all the required packages (I think?) when I restart Emacs. Also pretty necessary.

#+name: auto-package-update-use-package-declaration
#+begin_src elisp
  (use-package auto-package-update
               :ensure t
               :config
               (setq auto-package-update-delete-old-versions t)
               (setq auto-package-update-hide-results t)
               (auto-package-update-maybe))
#+end_src

This is my backup configuration, and I'm... not sure if it works. I mean, right now if I look in my local directory, there's an autosave of this file. Let's see, I think I know how to diagnose this from within /Emacs/. If I put the point (my cursor) right after the last character in =auto-save-mode=, I can hit =C-h v ENT= and look up the variable... which isn't documented. I guess that doesn't mean it didn't exist, though, but it seems unlikely it would be undocumented. So it probably is the wrong variable name. And also... this no longer seems like it'd be compatible with how I'd like to handle this sort of data-protection. I do want autosaves, in the same (or a predictable subdirectory) as the source file, and I don't necessarily want backups, because I should be using versioning control.

#+begin_src elisp :exports code
  (setq auto-save-mode nil)
  (setq backup-directory-alist
        `(("." . ,(concat user-emacs-directory "backups"))))
#+end_src elisp

This will need some work.
****** CANCELLED Evaluate-At-Point Tweak
   Currently if I'm in an Org-mode buffer - or maybe any buffer, I can hit the hotkey =C-x C-e= to evaluate whatever is before it; run it through the /Elisp REPL/. This is handy some of the time, but other times I mention a variable inside a pair of ===, and the interpretter complains that there's no variable ==foo==, even though there is a variable =foo=.
****** Workaround: Use Inline Src Calls
Inline source blocks, done with =src_elisp()=, are more explicit about what language they are, and caxn be evaluated just fine. This creates a new issue: those inline source blocks aren't rendered in a fixed-width font, if I'm using /Poet Theme/ and have src_elisp[](variable-pitch-mode) turned on, then they aren't fixed-width. Understandable, but it'd be cool if they were!

Here's an answer from [[https://stackoverflow.com/questions/20309842/how-to-syntax-highlight-for-org-mode-inline-source-code-src-lang/28059832#28059832][StackOverflow]] that might work:

#+begin_src elisp
  (font-lock-add-keywords 'org-mode
                      '(("\\(src_\\)\\([^[{]+\\)\\(\\[:.*\\]\\){\\([^}]*\\)}"
                         (1 'org-code)
                         (2 'org-code)
                         (3 'org-code)
                         (4 'org-code))))
#+end_src

Doesn't seem too, and I'm unable to figure out why, right now.
****** Thoughts on Star Trek: Deep Space Nine, S03E11: "Past Tense, Part 1"
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: thoughts-on-star-trek-deep-space-nine-s03e11
:END:
A Starfleet vessel has entered orbit of Earth, while in the past, history has changed such that humanity never has its wake-up call that motivates us to seek, in earnest, a post-scarcity model of society.

There is no response on standard hailing channels, so,

"Try a non-Federation frequency."

"The only subspace signals I'm detecting ar efrom the vicinity of Alpha Centauri... and they're Romulan!"

Now, this is interesting to me, because Vulcans had warp drive during the 21st century - before the past was changed.

Vulcan is quite a bit closer to Earth than Romulus.

Where are the Vulcans?

I think the line implies: without humanity, Vulcans would've been conquered by Romulus.

****** Waking Up                                     :NativeCulture:personal:
Today I woke up feeling generally upset at the continuing genocide of /Native Americans/, and tried to braid my hair. I'm not very good at it so ended up pulling it out, but I've put on my bangles and done my /facemark/. Last week {{{mr-chan}}} suggested my haircut - or lack thereof - was perhaps dissuading potential clients, as I looked like a "hippie." I chose not to try and debate the point with him, but I'd like to make it more clear without debate that the long hair is an intentional choice, not a consequence of ennui.

****** Call from {{{mr-chan}}}                                :TrusteeCorner:
I received a call from {{{mr-chan}}} a bit ago. He was disappointed with how I handled a recent issue, where a client's actions caused a vendor to leave an under unfulfilled, and sought to explain to me how I could better resolve the issue next time. He emphasized that part of why he was bothering was because he regreted that he had not had a similar influence earlier in his life. While the specifics of the vendor-issue were resolved adequately and in advance of any issue, from my perspective, I really appreciate that he takes the time to talk to me.

****** Web Developers Can't Pass the Buck
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: web-developers-cant-pass-the-buck
:END:
There's this thread going around the Fediverse about how metrics in web performance are bunk, but how it's excusable because academics hadn't yet demonstrated their bunkness.

Imagine if Boeing secured a thousand contracts to build a certain plane, and then when it turned out not to fly went, "Hey, it's on the public sector universities to tell us how to do business, this ain't our fault."

If you build a business - or orient your professional self-education - around a certain premise being true, and you sell people services that to implement that premise to give them the result...

...It's 100% on you for that. First off, where did you get the notion it was true? Clearly not from academics, so why is it their fault that it's wrong?

I guess my abstract point here is:

Don't do what the private sector tells you to and then blame the public sector for not warning you it's wrong or protecting you from the consequences.

Especially not if the things you're doing entitle you to one of the highest incomes a labourer can get. I won't have much sympathy for your ignorance; you had every means to check your assumptions before you sunk capital into developing them.

****** Thoughts on Merlin
:PROPERTIES:

:END:
God, I forgot how much more short the last season of BBC's Merlin falls so short of the others. The pacing gets weirder, the fight scenes get way... I want to say "campier" but they were already camp. They become bad-campy.

And weirdly, King Arthur's chainmail goes from like, nice-looking coats to stuff that looks like it's made of washers from the hardware store, while everyone else keeps the nice-looking stuff.

I dunno if it's to show he's king and so his armor is more about aesthetic?

To explain the last bit, Arthur is prince for most of the show and becomes king I think halfway through season 4. And then season 5 picks up 3 years into his reign, and most of the episodes have like, 3-5 year gaps (though it's never said how long, explicitly) between them. Which also feels weird, because like, King Arthur at the end of his reign looks about a year and a half older (because that's how much older the actor is) than Prince Arthur, in his teens, looked.

****** Is Developer Bias an Infringement of Free Software
So, I have a question. Is there any real difference between what tusky's doing, of treating a specific url in the code to a rickroll, and when a browser has redirects to their own pages builtin? I get the intent is different, deplatforming versus marketing, but source code is code, and doesn't carry intent.

I don't really see there as being a difference, but I've never heard anyone argue that builtin promote-thyself links are against the spirit of free software.

Like I just... I don't get the argument. Free software has no guarantee of fitness for a particular utility. That's not one of explicit four freedoms but it's a cultural thing we all appreciate, that software isn't "bad" because it doesn't meet every use. that's fine, because you can just change it to be more fit, right? Like...

I guess I'm asking, is this a grey area in the philosophy of free software, or misconstruing it? I think the latter, but I don't know.

****** Found a Cool /Elisp/ Declaration
*/Note to readers:/* A lot of what I'm working on here has to do with how my client looks to me, not how any rendered documents look. I may say something is working, when from your perspective, it isn't.

While looking up how I prettify my /Org-mode checkboxes/, I found that /Emacs/, since version 24.4, comes with a bultin =prettify-symbols-mode=. It swaps out text with a /Unicode/ symbol. This led me to a post [[http://www.modernemacs.com/post/prettify-mode/]["Mathematical Notation in Emacs",]] which is convenient for me: I want to learn to read maths.

I'm not going to rewrite its explanation of its code blocks, but reproduce them here for my own use. The first enables the =pretty-mode= package and turns off some parts of it, to make room for features that will be enabled by later code blocks.

#+begin_src elisp
(require 'pretty-mode)
(global-pretty-mode t)

(pretty-deactivate-groups
 '(:equality :ordering :ordering-double :ordering-triple
             :arrows :arrows-twoheaded :punctuation
             :logic :sets))

(pretty-activate-groups
 '(:sub-and-superscripts :greek :arithmetic-nary))
#+end_src

Let me see if I can't figure out how to write that as a =use-package= declaration, and since I'm not setting up the litagure stuff right now, I'll leave all the stuff on.

#+name: pretty-mode-use-package-declaration-alpha
#+begin_src elisp :noweb yes
  (use-package pretty-mode
    :ensure pretty-mode
    :config
    (progn
      (global-pretty-mode t)
      (global-prettify-symbols-mode 1)
      (pretty-activate-groups
       '(:sub-and-superscripts :greek :arithmetic-nary))))
#+end_src

That seems to work! (And now gets appended to my /Emacs configuration/.

The article also has another quote introducing some /Emacs commands/ I didn't know about:

#+begin_quote
The command =insert-char=, =C-x 8 RET= prompts for Unicode characters by name and inserts at point. There is also =describe-char=, =M-m h d c= which gives information on the character at point. Use in conjunction with eg. [[https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_operators_and_symbols_in_Unicode]["Math Unicode Symbols List"]] to explore your options.
#+end_quote

Nifty! Looking elsewhere, it looks like the only thing that is prettified in lisp dialects is the lambda symbol, and it suggests things can be added like this:

#+begin_src elisp
(add-hook 'emacs-lisp-mode-hook
            (lambda ()
              (push '(">=" . ?≥) prettify-symbols-alist)))
#+end_src

Which, testing out, works just fine. I wouldn't mind mentions if the whole greek alphabet got replaced, and in Org-mode too - though there I'd do it with a prefix of =\= since that's the LaTeX for it. I encounter a problem here, though: I don't actually understand the syntax above, so I don't know how I'd change it to add multiple things to the =prettify-symbols-alist=.

I guess what I need to know is what's a \lambda expression in /Emacs/, and what's the =push= function takes and returns.

I was reading about \lambda calculus on /Wikipedia/ recently, so the /Elisp Reference Manual's/ brief explanation of what \lambda expressions might make sense. It says they're "anonymous functions," but are more commonly associated with symbols to make a named function. A \lambda expression - contained within =()=, always starts with the =lambda= symbol, then takes a list of argument variables, called the "\lambda list." There can be a documentation string, an interactive declaration, and then the body - very similar to a normal function declaration, except with =lambda= instead of =defun function-name=.

=push= I looked up with =C-h f push ENT=, it adds its first argument to the list stored at its second argument, so =(push "apple" '("orange" "banana" "berry"))=... gets me an error about "orange" not being a function. ='("orange" "banana" "berry")= is a valid list declaration, so why does =push= fail?...

#+begin_src elisp
  (setq fruits '("orange" "banana" "berry"))
  (push '"apple" fruits)
#+end_src

Okay - I'm messing - oh! I was messing up declaring the list. Let's combine two things, maybe?

#+begin_src elisp
  (push '"apple" (lambda () '("orange" "banana" "berry")))
#+end_src

That gets me this error message, that I have a real hard time reading: =let*: Symbol’s function definition is void: \(setf\ function\)=.

I think that's still telling me "this second argument isn't a function," which seems to contra- oh! One more try:

#+begin_src elisp
  (push '"apple" '(lambda () '("orange" "banana" "berry")))
#+end_src

Nope - I added a ='= before the \lambda expression, but get a similar error. Is =lambda= perhaps an /Elisp macro/ for =(setf function)=, or similar?

Either way, I don't need to figure out the more compact syntax right now: I'm still looking to add all the greek letters at once to the Org- and Elisp- modes of =prettify-symbols=. Lets play with some fake code. And actually, I'm going to try this =let= thing I've heard of.

#+begin_src elisp
  (let ((alpha '("oh..." . "it rendered as a greek character"))))
#+end_src

So I guess no further configuration needed for that. I guess the example of =>== to =≥= is still relevant, though, so I can chug on with this.

#+begin_src elisp
  (let ((alpha '(1 2 3))
        (beta '(4 5 6)))
    (dolist (gamma beta)
      (push gamma alpha))
    alpha)
#+end_src

This gives me a list of =(6 5 4 1 2 3)=, which shows that push puts things at the start of a list. That's not relevant to my goals, but does give me a hypothetical syntax for my need:

#+name: math-litagures-elisp-prettify-hook
#+begin_src elisp :noweb yes
  (add-hook 'emacs-lisp-mode-hook
            (lambda ()
              (let ((replacements
                     '((">=" . ?≥))))
                (dolist (alpha replacements)
                  (push alpha prettify-symbols-alist)))))
#+end_src

And now if I put =>== in an /Elisp/ source block, it should become the litagure. (That's the name for two characters combined like this.)

#+begin_src elisp
  >=
#+end_src

(This is a rendering thing, so any readers of~ an export will likely not see anything functional.)

Okay, so I think now I have two source blocks I want to add to my /Emacs configuration/:

#+begin_src elisp :noweb yes
<<pretty-mode-use-package-declaration-alpha>>
<<math-litagures-elisp-prettify-hook>>
#+end_src

Since the the latter relies on the =pretty-mode= package, I should refactor them together, and go ahead and in more symbols:

#+name: pretty-mode-use-package-declaration-beta
#+begin_src elisp
  (use-package pretty-mode
    :ensure pretty-mode
    :config
    (progn
      (global-pretty-mode t)
      (global-prettify-symbols-mode 1)
      (pretty-activate-groups
       '(:greek :logic-nary :sets-operations-nary :sub-and-super-scripts))
      (add-hook 'emacs-lisp-mode-hook
                (lambda ()
                  (let ((replacements
                         '((">=" . ?≥)
                           ("<=" . ?≤)
                           ("defun" . ?ƒ)
                           ("for" . ?∀)))
                    (dolist (alpha replacements)
                      (push alpha prettify-symbols-alist))))))))
#+end_src

At this point I need to take a break and do some other stuff, but I feel like this was a good exercise in learning to work with more /Elisp/ stuff.

Now I can write code that looks like this:

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun ems-tmp-accel
      (alpha beta gamma delta)
     (/ (- beta alpha) (- delta gamma)))
#+end_src

Which just looks cool, even if the example is a bit contrived.

****** Mastodon and "NSFW"
This is recorded anonymously with permission, because I didn't know about this and expect others won't either. And, contrary to the title, this may just be an issue with /Pleroma/, not /Mastodon/.

#+begin_quote
Did you know mastodon “removed” the nsfw button because of it having a lot of problems, but only in the UI?

That's right, the backend still gets a lot of mileage out of the phrase! It's used for sensitive content in a manner that is HIDDEN from users, not even knowing that their posts will federate to things that don't support the weird, hidden hashtag, by adding it in plain sight

It still functionally exists, too! The reason it was added as an invisible hashtag was for “backwards compatibility”, with ostatus I believe, a software that... no longer sees much use, and was primarily so that users could mute it to not see posts of that variety

But by that logic, any tag works.

Like.

Like “sensitive content”, the phrase that was switched to because users argued about “nsfw” being anti-sex worker, enforcing a certain type of shitty social norm... etcetc

But nah

We'll just leave that hash tag in there, hidden but still very present, encouraging usage of the term

Don't believe me? Use your instance to search “#nsfw” and you'll find a bunch of posts that DONT have that hashtag in it, anywhere

even... though they show up under the search

because it's hidden, in the post, because that post is marked as “sensitive content”
#+end_quote

****** Adding Olivetti Mode
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: adding-olivetti-mode
:END:
I'm adding /olivetti-mode/ to my /Emacs configuration/, with a hook to turn it on by default for /Org-mode/. I might also turn it on for /Elisp/ - since whitespace there doesn't matter, I've even been considering migrating away from a fixed-width font. But, for now, I'm sticking with it.

Here's the /use-package/ declaration:

#+name olivetti-mode-use-package-declaration
#+begin_src elisp
(use-package olivetti
    :ensure olivetti
    :after org
    :config
    (progn
      (setq olivetti-body-width 0.75)
      (add-hook 'org-mode-hook 'olivetti-mode)))
#+end_src

Simple enough.
****** Literally Programming a Fantasy World?
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: literately-programming-a-fantasy-world
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2019-06-19 Wed 17:55]--[2019-06-19 Wed 17:55] =>  0:00
:END:
I wrote, three days ago now, that I was going to use a single /Org-mode/ file that I was going to write from. I've been doing a better job of that than I expected to, but I also haven't been able to spend much time on a computer, so that doesn't count for much.

One thing I've been doing - though I don't know if anyone has noticed. (I'm checking, but I can only see the top 25 posts I've put on my /WriteFreely/ account.) Anyway, one thing I've been doing is putting the non-private sections of this file's daily section on my /WriteFreely/ blog, currently at [[https://posts.emsenn.net/][https://posts.emsenn.net]] - if you're reading this and it's only a few days after now, and not me, that's probably where. Hi, anonymous reader!

So that's a kind of cool side-effect of this. I'll probably run the output through /Hugo/ soon too, to make it a more robust site.

Anyway, I was wondering what I might be able to do to handle writing and adding to my fantasy canon and the resource books and all that stuff I want to do. I've written about that enough other times in the past, but that's out of the context of this sort of log file, so I'll cover it again very briefly: /Teraum/ is a fantasy setting I've been writing in basically my whole life, and I want to make a bunch of media set in it, from stories and podcasts to table-top role-playing game resources.

One thing I realized that I'll have to accept about /literate programming/ is that it's okay to rewrite and edit what came before - the previous sections in this document can be changed, if that suits my needs. I know that sounds obvious, but it was a mental hangup I had, that once I wrote something, getting rid of it was... bad.
******* Background

Anyway, so, the very first time I wanted to "codify" my fantasy setting it was as a /multi-user dimension/, or /MUD/. This seemed easy-enough to me, as I played /MUDs/ at the time. I played /LPC MUDs/, which means that in-game things, like /NPCs/ and rooms in buildings or sections of streets, were objects, with attributes and states. It's been a bit since I've done Python, but here's a mockup:

#+begin_src python
  class Tavern(object):
      def __init__:
          super.__init__(self)
          self.name = "Twisted Alembic"
          self.description = ("The Twisted Alembic clearly was a nice "
                              "tavern, but these days it's a bit more "
                              "rundown.")
          self.exits = { "south" : "caliber_street" }
#+end_src

Something like that, at least.
******* Serializing the Twisted Alembic in Elisp
Now thinking about it I can pretty easily make a similar thing as an elisp declaration:

#+name: teraum-twisted-alembic-declaration
#+caption: An elisp declaration to set the =teraum-twisted-alembic= variable.
#+begin_src elisp
  (setq teraum-twisted-alembic
        '(("name" . "Twisted Alembic")
          ("description" . "The Twisted Alembic clearly was a nice
          tavern, but these days it's a bit more rundown.")
          ("exits"
           (("south" .  teraum-caliper-street)))))
#+end_src

I did something similar with my /personal dictionary/, though I stopped tinkering with that once I learned the basics of the syntax.

I like this more too because rather than a string that requires a lookup (or possible recursion, or needing to load all the variables into memory, if I understand /Python/ right,) I can just reference the variable and get a =nil= if I call to it like with =(cdr (assoc "south" (cdr (assoc "exits" teraum-twisted-alembic))))=.
******* Serializing Caliper Street
So, that record references a "Caliper Street," which I'll need to define, too.

#+name: teraum-caliper-street-declaration
#+caption: An elisp declaration to set the =teraum-caliper-street= variable.
#+begin_src elisp
  (setq teraum-caliper-street
        '(("name" . "Caliper Street")
          ("description" . "One of the busiest streets in the Brass
          Ward, Caliper Street is home to the workshops that drive
          Ack's more luxurious industries. Bookbinders, luthiers,
          watchmakers and more have shops here.")
          ("exits"
           ("north" .  teraum-twisted-alembic))))
#+end_src

*/Note:/* at this point I had to take quite a bit of time learning more about /Elisp/ syntax, because I was failing to be able to refer to a list as a variable inside another list. The thing I needed was the =symbol-value= function.

So here's a little bit of code that returns the name of the northern exit of Caliper Street - that is, it returns ="Twisted Alembic"=.

#+caption: An elisp declaration to read the north exit from the =teraum-caliper-street= variable.
#+begin_src elisp
  (cdr (assoc "name" (symbol-value (cdr (assoc "north" (cdr (assoc "exits" teraum-caliper-street)))))))
#+end_src

And here's that done a little bit more programmatically, though not well, just for the practice of it.

#+caption: Another elisp declaration to read the north exit from the =teraum-caliper-street= variable.
#+begin_src elisp
  (let ((alpha teraum-caliper-street) (beta "exits") (gamma "north")) (cdr (assoc "name" (symbol-value (cdr (assoc gamma (cdr (assoc beta alpha))))))))
#+end_src

And here's that same thing, done using a \lambda expression, just because I think those are cool.

#+caption: Yet another elisp declaration to read the north exit from the =teraum-caliper-street= variable.
#+begin_src elisp
  (funcall (lambda (alpha beta) (cdr (assoc "name" (symbol-value (cdr (assoc beta (cdr (assoc "exits" alpha)))))))) teraum-caliper-street "north")
#+end_src
******* Writing Some Teraum Functions

Now, I recognize that those are both pretty verbose, but I think it's cool how they can be written pretty arbitrarily, and I can check where I am in any part of it with /Emacs/ builtin /REPL/. Plus, I can write functions:

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun teraum-get-name
      (subject)
    "Returns the proper name of the SUBJECT within Teraum."
    (cdr (assoc "name" subject)))
  (defun teraum-get-desc
      (subject)
    "Returns the description of the SUBJECT within Teraum."
    (cdr (assoc "description")))
#+end_src

Those two are simple enough. I wrote another kind of by rote that I'll share, but then I'm going to talk about what I've written so far.

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun teraum-get-exits
      (subject)
    "Returns the exits from the SUBJECT within Teraum."
    (cdr (assoc "exits")))
#+end_src
******* Another Go At Serializing

Before I go further, I think I want to change how I'm storing these records. Rather than each one its own variable, I think I'm going to declare a =teraum-data= variable, and have each one be an element of that list. Also, =exits= is kind of a traditional name from /MUDs/, and I think I'd rather have some a more generic term, like =nav=, since this'll translate across scales, not just rooms and segments of a road. So let's refactor our data-structure a bit.

#+begin_src elisp
  (setq teraum-data
        '((caliper-street
           (name . "Caliper Street")
           (description . "This is Caliper Street.")
           (nav
            (114 . twisted-alembic)))
          (twisted-alembic
           (name . "Twisted Alembic")
           (description . "This is the Twisted Alembic.")
           (nav
            (south . caliper-street)))))
#+end_src

There's a lot more arbitrary symbol use because, well, I honestly forgot that was a thing I could do instead of quoting them!
******* And Another Go At Functions
So, now I need to take a fresh look at the functions

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun teraum-read-record
      (subject)
    "Returns the list representing the record of SUBJECT."
    (cdr (assoc subject teraum-data)))
  (defun teraum-read-name
      (subject)
    "Returns the name of the SUBJECT."
    (cdr (assoc 'name (teraum-read-record subject))))
  (defun teraum-read-desc
      (subject)
    "Returns the description of the SUBJECT."
    (cdr (assoc 'description (teraum-read-record subject))))
  (defun teraum-read-nav
      (subject)
    "Returns the nav items of the SUBJECT."
    (cdr (assoc 'nav (teraum-read-record subject))))
  (defun teraum-read-nav-item
      (subject item)
    "Returns the record of the ITEM in the SUBJECT's navigation list."
    (cdr (assoc item (teraum-read-nav subject))))
#+end_src

That's a longer code block than I'm used to doing in this sort of log entry, but, it all kind of follows each other.
******* Serializing the Green Delta
So now rather than write about a region in English I can just do

#+begin_src elisp
  (push
   '(green-delta
     (name . "Green Delta")
     (description . "The Green Delta is a fertile region, dense with farms and small villages.")
     (nav
      (east . central-plains)
      (southeast . gloaming)
      (south . widewoods)
      (west . optic-ocean)))
   teraum-data)
#+end_src

And give a bit of information about the /Green Delta/. Of course I imagine down the line I'll want to have functions that better let me add stuff, with some sort of safeguards that it's the right sort of data, maybe? I suppose I do know how to read the errors it might throw though, so I shouldn't stretch out of my way to write writer-functions that aren't actually helpful, I suppose.
******* Serializing Teraum's Easy-Smoking Pipe
Now I'd like to try and write about something real. I'm going to start with something simple, the /easy-smoking pipe/, which a magic item currently held by /Jenos Anadini/. (Maybe I'll get to him, next.)

For this,

#+begin_src elisp
  (push
   '(easy-smoking-pipe
     (name . "easy-smoking pipe")
     (description . "The easy-smoking pipe is a wooden pipe which emits an intoxicating smoke. The magic comes from the pipe itself; anything burnt in it produces the same effect.")
     (location . anadini-jenos))
   teraum-data)
#+end_src
******* A Function to Read Locations
This introduces a new kind of... key?, =location=. So, should add some matching functions, or at least the one to read it.

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun teraum-read-location
      (subject)
    "Returns the location of SUBJECT."
    (cdr (assoc 'location (teraum-read-record subject))))
#+end_src
******* Serializing Jenos Anadini
And so that we aren't left hanging, let's give some sort of definition to /Jenos Anadini/. My thought is, if I define him poorly and sparsely right now, I can learn some more /Elisp/ syntax to try and properly flesh him out.

#+begin_src elisp
  (push
   '(anadini-jenos
     (name . "Jenos Anadini")
     (description . "Jenos Anadini is a middle-aged human man who works for Honeyfern Laboratory as an archivist.")
     (inventory
      (easy-smoking-pipe .)))
   teraum-data)
#+end_src
******* Adding An Event
So let's see, what else might be in his record. I suppose I could add a bit about his history; maybe a list of events?

#+begin_src elisp
  (push
   '(43pc
     (Summer
      (21
       (concat
          "In "
          (teraum-read-name 'pled)
          ", "
          (teraum-read-name 'anadini-jenos)
          " is born."))))
   teraum-data)
#+end_src

So here I'm using the year as the main record key (I still don't know if that's the proper term, with a list for each month, containing a list for each day, that contains a list of strings for each event. You can see it's reading from two records to make that string. (For the curious, and myself if I forget: =(teraum-read-name '43pc)= returns =nil=, which is conveniently truthful and non-error-y.

So, the issue with this format is that every event needs to have a known day, but since it's fiction, I can just make that up when I need to.
******* Function for Reading a Day's Event
With events in the record, I need a function for reading them, too.

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun teraum-read-events
      (year month day)
    "Returns a list of the events that happened on DAY of MONTH of YEAR."
    (loop
     for event
     in (cdr (assoc day (cdr (assoc month (teraum-read-record year)))))
      collect event))
#+end_src

Now I'm having a bit of an issue working with the list that returns in such a way that it processes the =concat= that's embedded in that, so I'll have to get that sorted, but I need to take a break for now.
******* Further Reading about Literately Programming A Fantasy World

After sharing my post about literately programming a fantasy world, there were recommendations for other related reading which I will link to here:

           ("Creating Languages in Racket" (author . "Matthew Flat")

****** Blocklists on the Fediverse
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: blocklists-on-the-fediverse
:END:
There's a mobile phone application for accessing /Mastodon/ instances, to post to and read content from the /Fediverse/. The app is called /Tusky/ and recently they patched the software so that users from a certain white supremacist instance would be redirected to an irrelevant video, essentially blocking them for using the app with that instance.

There's a lot of argument around that, which I might get to, but I've seen a few people say that the whole argument could be avoided with shared blocklists - a configuration shared publicly that would let instances basically follow another instance's instance moderation.

These sorts of blocklists exist for users on /Twitter/, and they're viewed as highly problematic, despite having started with identical intentions: to alleviate the difficulty in removing white supremacist voices from one's view.

In fact, last spring, or maybe summer, /Wil Wheaton/ was collectively run-off the /Fediverse/ by coordinated harassment, explicitly because of his (claimed accidental) misuse of blocklists.

Explicitly, the risk is that marginalized people are less able to appeal being added to such lists, since they are generally in possession of less social capital, and online discourse tends to side against them by default.

It's interesting to me that the same people who helped coordinate harassing /Wheaton/ off the /Fediverse/ are now advocating for a similar tool as what he misused, without seeming to appreciate that developing such tools would enable again such misuse.

Being explicit though, I have no problem with allowlists, or whitelists, where list of not who to block, but of which instances are allowed, being shared. I view that as a mimic of how social interaction has worked for much of our past. I mean, you can only interact with an entity after you know it exists, right? Whereas with the /Fediverse's/ model, you interact - in the sense of /broadcasting/ a message to them - by anyone who has an instance.

There's a lot of different social communication that comes up with the /Fediverse/, and I have to think that a lot of them would be solved by a whitelist-y model. I know that'd introduce a lot of other problems, but I really do think that /Mastodon/, and so the rest of the /Fediverse/, are letting themselves being wagged by the golden goose of "discoverability," when that's never been important outside of advertising. (Yes, I mixed a metaphor, a tail wagging a dog and a goose laying a golden egg.)

****** Get an Item from My Pending Reading List
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: get-an-item-from-my-pending-reading-list
      :END:
Just a bit ago I set up an =ems-reading-list= variable, and I'd like to set up a function to get a random item from those items in the =pending= sublist.

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun ems-read-something
      ()
    "Return a random element's cdr, which should be a string that is a URL, from the ems-reading-list variable."
    (cdr (assoc 'source (cdr (seq-random-elt (cdr (assoc 'pending ems-reading-list)))))))
  (defun ems-read-something-now ()
    "Opens a random item from the pending reading list."
    (interactive) (browse-url (ems-read-something)))
#+end_src

Now, I can do =M-x ems-read-something-now= and the browser will immediately open up something in my reading list. Next is to make a function for adding something to the reading list, and moving it to a =read= section.

****** Playing with Thorns in Org-mode

I'm going to copy my existing /use-package/ declaration for /pretty-mode/ and add to that - I'm still not comfortable working with lists and it's about 0200h and I don't feel like trying to learn... anything.

-----

It's now the morning. I put an =:after org= in this as well as the new config. It works, but the problem is symbols only count if they're a "word" by themselves - th shows as a thorn, but the shows as t h e.

In the light of day, I'm realizing this is probably not a thing worth doing. What might be more interesting is to have words replaced with their /international phonetic alphabet/ equivalent. I've always been annoyed I don't know how to read it, and having the word "the" replaced by "ði."

That said, even for "the," it requires some figuring to know if it's "thuh" or "thee," so maybe this whole thing is best abandoned for now. In fact; I shall just drop it.
****** Thoughts on Free Software Developers
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: thoughts-on-free-software-developers
      :END:
I'm noticing huge chunks of the free-software world is largely ignorant to the philosophical motivates behind free software, and view their participation in it more as participation in a sort of majority-run corporation, which... makes sense in hindsight, and explains a lot of the contradiction I see in people's views.

They aren't rejecting property rights, they're trying to become a part of the "winning" structure by hedging their bets outside proprietary software. Because of the white academic roots of free- and open-source software, I think a lot of participants don't realize that most of what they do reinforces that as the dominant voice in the field, and don't appreciate that all those issues of equality meaning little without equity follow them from social pub debates to their actions adjacent to free software.

I've been thinking about this because in response to a few /microblog/ posts I made saying so, people told me free software was not a rejection of intellectual property rights.

And it's interesting to me as someone who's sided with open-, not free-, source, even after giving it some recent consideration. I think the open-source community is ignorant too, and their actions denote a similar amount of ignorance to their roots, but generally I view open-source people as less philosophically pedantic, so I'm a bit more forgiving of their contradictions.
****** Commits Since Documentation Update Disclosure

I wonder if there's a documentation system that tracks how many commits have affected the documented code and presents that in the rendering; "There have been 5 commits affecting these functions since the documentation was changed."
****** Do New Web Communication Services Fail Because They're Object Oriented?
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: do-new-web-communication-services-fail-because-theyre-object-oriented
      :END:
This might be wrong because I'm new to the concepts but I feel like we treat a lot of doing stuff online in an object-oriented way, each service holds a bit of us an object and those all interact in different ways and we tap into the messaging and side-effects to get useful stuff.

Whereas some stuff, like (not webclient) email, is more functional, where the input and output are files on your computer, and the service just exists to do operations on them.

And I wonder if part of the conceptual struggle to build better Internet communication systems isn't because the people working to build those systems are, because they're competing for market share (because they want grants and shit, at least), building them inside this object-y framework, so they can't actually address the problems that are (might be, I'm hypothesising) a consequence of that framework.

****** Emacs Autosave Configuration
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: emacs-autosave-configuration
      :END:
The last time I looked at my /Emacs configuration/ I stopped with the auto-save and backup configuration, because I realized how I have done in the past didn't really reflect how I'd like to do it in the future.

I want auto-saves, though probably prefixed with a dot not hash, but I don't need backups, since I use git.

I'd like to try and learn how to better-configure this - at least the autosave part - just from within /Emacs/, by reading the manual. There are a few things in the manual that interest me,.

First, it says that the autosave thing of starting with a # is a bit programmatic, so I'll leave it alone. Second, I could just autosave directly into the file with an =auto-save-visited-mode= but it looks like it autosaves every five seconds of idleness, not based on file length, which would really quickly overload my little laptop. So I guess I'll just leave autosaves alone!

And, reading further, backups are turned off if /Emacs/ can see the file is under /version control/, so I needn't configure anything with that, either. So, let's see what bits of /Emacs configuration/ I've logged about this week:

#+name: emacs-config-bits
#+begin_src elisp
<<package-initialize-declaration>>
<<package-source-declarations>>
<<auto-package-update-use-package-declaration>>
<<pretty-mode-use-package-declaration-beta>>
<<olivetti-mode-use-package-declaration>>
#+end_src

It's now just after midnight, so I'm going to call that an end to this bit of logging.

****** Function to Plan to Read Something
      :PROPERTIES:

A little bit ago I manually pushed something into my new pending reading list, but now I'd like to give a shot at writing a simple function - and command - for that purpose. This'll be my first use building a list like this.

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun ems-plan-to-read
      (tit auth src)
    "Adds an item to my reading list"
    (interactive "sTitle? \nsAuthor? \nsSource? ")
    (push
     (cons tit (cons (cons 'author auth) (list (cons 'source src))))
     (cdr (assoc 'pending ems-reading-list))))
#+end_src
****** Response to John Ankarstrom's "Writing HTML in HTML"
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: response-to-john-ankarstroms-writing-html-in-html
      :END:
A few days ago I wrote and responded briefly to a piece; I'm adding those notes here. The piece was John Ankarstrom's [[http://john.ankarstrom.se/html/]["Writing HTML in HTML"]]. I immediately disagree with his reasoning, even though I agree with handwriting HTML. A static site generator /adds/ a layer of redirection between you and your /layout/. It /removes/ a layer of redirection between you and your /content/. Also, he straight-up advocates copying boilerplate and that way lies madness. One shouldn't copy a boilerplate framework but add boilerplate to a content, or else you end up with more and more legacy detritus.  I don't wanna pile on but his site has like a half-dozen pages and at least a couple broken links, which is a "time capsule" but eh, that's what the back entries in a git repo are for!

#+begin_src elisp
  (push
   '(read
     ("Writing HTML in HTML" (author . "John Ankarstrom")
      (source . "http://john.ankarstrom.se/html/")))
   ems-reading-list)
#+end_src

****** Thoughts on the Watershed of the Internet
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: thoughts-on-the-watershed-of-the-internet
      :END:
There's a twitter thread someone may be able to find that discusses how unlike many fields, computer science has not had its "reckoning," the way physics did with the atomic bomb or chemistry did with chemical warfare, and as such, ethics is not a part of its education the way first year physics students have to learn about the ethics of their prospective field.

I'm wondering what what the watershed event will be - have we had it and we don't recognize it? had it and it's still kept secret from us? or still to come? I would've hoped the /Christchurch mosque shooting/ would've been it, but that seems to have been forgotten as fast as anything else.

When we look back at this, will the watershed event - the /Kent State shooting/, the /immolation of Mohamed Bouazizi/, the /410 Sack of Rome/, it's early and I can't namedrop more history - have already happened and it's an event we know of, or has it happened and we don't know it has yet, or is it yet to come.

Not to nitpick my point but I feel like y'all are treating it like a silly question for no reason, but if it's either of the first two, then that means it's something we can do something about, by putting it into the crucible of public attention and amplifying it as a symbol of the cultural decisions being made.

To make this more specific, when /Sedazad/ focused on the death of a single protester as an important thing, even though other protesters were dying and in larger volume, we were able to greatly increase the "marketing" we were able to generate for ourselves, and thus funding and effort.
****** Thoughts on Writing Code to Log
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: thoughts-on-writing-code-to-log
      :END:
Writing bespoke little bits of elisp to track something like my reading list is silly and flawed in a lot of ways, and will never have some of the most basic features a more official program would, but it's also trivial to build things the way I'd prefer from the ground up. For example, when I read a thing, I'd like to store a rating for it, but not just a one-of-ten: I'd like to rate something based on a few different metrics and take the average of them. I'm not entirely sure /what/ I'd like metrics I'd like to track, or how to phrase it. I'd like to take some measure of a piece's utility or fitness, in contrast to its style - there's a lot of content (especially online content like blog posts) that I like the ideas in, but not so much the writing style.

I'm trying to remember what the five points of the "five-point rubric" I recall from secondary school is. Searching online reveals it was not five points to be measured, but a measure of one in five, how fit is the piece of writing. I think for now I might use two measures: style and content. Writing this, I happened to think of one post I really enjoy the content of, but not so much the style: "Kirk Drift" by  Erin Horáková.

#+begin_src elisp

   '("Kirk Drift" (author . "Erin Horáková")
    (source . "http://strangehorizons.com/non-fiction/columns/freshly-rememberd-kirk-drift/")
    (rating (style . 4) (content . 8)))
   (cdr (assoc 'read ems-reading-list)))
#+end_src

****** Refreshing My Chromebook
This morning I wiped my laptop, a /Chromebook/, because its drive was full of useless crap. I'm going to attempt to install /Guix/ on it, because also my /Emacs/ install was only version 25, and I'd rather move on to 26. Here's how to get started: install =xz-utils= so I can decompress archives, download the /Guix/ binary for my architecture, and make sure the file I have matches the file I meant to get.

#+begin_src sh
apt install xz-utils
cd ~ && mkdir src
wget https://ftp.gnu.org/gnu/guix/guix-binary-1.0.1.armhf-linux.tar.xz
wget https://ftp.gnu.org/gnu/guix/guix-binary-1.0.1.armhf-linux.tar.xz.sig
gpg --keyserver pool.sks-keyservers.net --recv-keys 3CE464558A84FDC69DB40CFB090B11993D9AEBB5
gpg --verify guix-binary-1.0.1.system.tar.xz.sig
#+end_src

That returns that the signature is good, so I can move on confident I'm working with an uncorrupt binary. The next set of commands is:

#+begin_src sh
tar --warning=no-timestamp -xf guix-binary-1.0.1.armhf-linux.tar.xz
#+end_src

Then I do =sudo su= to switch to my root user (within the Linux virtual machine my Chromebook has, and then =mv var/guix /var/ && mv gnu /= to create =/var/guix=/ and =/gnu/store=.  The former has my root profile, which I'll install:

#+begin_src sh
mkdir -p ~root/.config/guix
ln -sf /var/guix/profiles/per-user/root/current-guix ~root/.config/guix/current
GUIX_PROFILE="`echo ~root`/.config/guix/current" ; source $GUIX_PROFILE/etc/profile
#+end_src

The next step is adding a group for guix so it can build stuff

#+begin_src sh
groupadd --system guixbuild
for i in `seq -w 1 10`;
  do
    useradd -g guixbuild -G guixbuild -d /var/empty -s `which nologin` -c "Guix build user $i" --system guixbuilder$i;
  done
#+end_src

This makes a usergroup and ten accounts for concurrent building? I guess, I'm just doing what it says. Next is adding the daemon; /Chromebook/'s /Linux/ virtual machine uses /Systemd/ so

#+begin_src sh
cp ~root/.config/guix/current/lib/systemd/system/guix-daemon.service /etc/systemd/system/
 systemctl start guix-daemon && systemctl enable guix-daemon
#+end_src

Now to make sure my user has access to the guix command:

#+begin_src sh
mkdir -p /usr/local/bin
cd /usr/local/bin
ln -s /var/guix/profiles/per-user/root/current-guix/bin/guix
#+end_src

The manual is also useful to have, as a user:

#+begin_src sh
mkdir -p /usr/local/share/info
cd /usr/local/share/info
for i in /var/guix/profiles/per-user/root/current-guix/share/info/* ;
  do ln -s $i ; done
#+end_src

Exiting back to my user, I don't have the guix command, /and/ the terminal crashes when I try to open it.

So let's get to chrome's terminal with ctrl+shift+t and run =vmc start debugalpha=  to start a container to run a vm in (the linux vm that has the terminal app).

from inside the vmc whatever, i run

run_container.sh --container_name=penguin --user=emsenn --shell

Which is either hanging or taking forever. Always hard to tell with chromebooks.

I'll go make a sandwich, but to be clear, this is all to install guix as the package manager on an otherwise stock chromebook, which is a silly and unnecessary task so hitting hurdles doesn't reflect poorly on guix.

Opening another terminal to look at top and my system's not busy.

So it's not lost to time, what triggered this is the channel my "apt" binary is looking to has emacs 25.something, and that doesn't have the "seq-random-elt" function that my "ems-read-something" function relies on, so I wanted to install emacs 26 but from a package manager, not source, because I don't wanna build emacs from source on a chromebook.

At this point, I'm taking a different tack: using /Flatpak/. I refreshed the /Chromebook/ again and then opened the terminal.

#+begin_src sh
sudo apt install flatpak
sudo flatpak remote-add --if-not-exists flathub https://flathub.org/repo/flathub.flatpakrepo
#+end_src

Then I went to /Flathub/ and downloaded the [[https://flathub.org/apps/details/org.gnu.emacs][Emacs pak]], moved it to my home directory in the Linux virtual machine, and ran =sudo flatpak install --from org.gnu.emacs.flatpakref=.

This, after a few moments, gets me /Emacs/ 26.2.
****** Installing IBM Plex Fonts on My Chromebook
The following commands download the /IBM Plex/ font repository into =~/src/plex/= and then links the regular fonts into =~/home/emsenn/.fonts/=. The =fc-cache -f= command forces a regeneration of the font cache, which makes the font show up for /Emacs/ and other programs.

#+begin opinion
The /IBM Plex/ font family is, by the way, my font family of choice. I think it has one of the most cohesive design philosophies of any contemporary font, and its one of very few that extends their philosophy so coherently across a monospace, sans-serif, and serif font. I also happen to agree with a lot of the design choices, and, importantly, think they look goood. My current /Emacs configuration/ uses monospace for code blocks, sans-serif for headlines, and serif for the body, so I frequently have all three visible on my screen, and I quite enjoy how they look.
#+end

#+begin_src sh
  cd ~/src
  git clone https://github.com/IBM/plex
  ln -s /home/emsenn/src/plex/IBM-Plex-Mono/fonts/complete/otf/IBMPlexMono-Regular.otf /home/emsenn/.fonts/
  ln -s /home/emsenn/src/plex/IBM-Plex-Sans/fonts/complete/otf/IBMPlexSans-Regular.otf /home/emsenn/.fonts/
  ln -s /home/emsenn/src/plex/IBM-Plex-Serif/fonts/complete/otf/IBMPlexSerif-Regular.otf /home/emsenn/.fonts/
fc-cache -f
#+end_src

****** Writing With Logs
I've been writing writing this log this way for five days now, and what I haven't managed to do is integrate any sort of longer form writing into it. Like, if I want to write an essay, how will that work? Also, what about my older work?

I think this is a thing worth considering, so as silly as it mmight seem, I'm going to take the time to just kind of write about it here. I have a small collection of /personal directives/, my rules to live by. Here are a few that seem immediately relevant to the business of writing:

- 002 :: Data should be private.
- 003 :: Useful information cannot be owned.
- 005 :: Figurative language is lazy.
- 006 :: Data should be parseable by humans.
- 008 :: No job is finished until the paperwork is complete.
- 012 :: Information should be atomic.
- 017 :: Only functional systems should be automated.
- 018 :: Useful information should be shared.

I have to take a break from this to clean. A thought while cleaning, code blocks could get built into compositions at the end of the document? So in addition ot the Logs section there's a Compositions section? Kind of a double entendre.

Then different drafts get labeled like, =essay-alpha=, or =essay-first-argument-beta=, and I can keep the source block under Compositions organized properly.

****** Thoughts on Upcoming ICE Raids
There's been reports that /Immigration and Customs Enforcement/ is going to be conducting mass immigrant round-ups on Sunday, and a lot of people in the Fediverse are sharing posts about it. I'm concerned some of them aren't considering the risks they're taking as American residents sharing anti-law enforcement sentiment in public channels. I know I publicly express that sentiment myself, but I'm already known to dislike law enforcement so the damage is done. A lot of these folk are young folk, even kids.
****** Sharing These Logs
It's been suggested to me that instead of sharing these logs as once-daily posts at [[https://posts.emsenn.net][https://posts.emsenn.net]], I share each one as it happens. I posted a poll and the early results indicate that'd be preferred.

However, to do that in a way I'd like, I think I'd need to define a bit of process for how to get the posts from my /Org-mode/ source file to the /Markdown/ that /WriteFreely/ wants - and, in a way that it's easy enough to update the post when I update the log entry. (Though I guess I can wait until I mark each log entry as done. Should I make each log entry start with draft and not just the ones that I know will take multiple sessions to write?)

See, a lot of questions. So for now I'm going to stick with the daily logs, but with plans to, in the future, switch to a more verbose per-entry post style. Which, on a given day, might be anywhere from 12-20 log entries, it seems like, which would be quite a prolific blog! Maybe /WriteFreely/ then isn't the best platform for that sort of thing, since it does seem more intended for longer-form writing than that. Not to say that some of these long entries don't get long.

So, the question. Is /WriteFreely/ the best platform for sharing these logs? To answer that, I need to define the most extreme conditions for an entry - the longest, the shortest, the most marked-up, most media-rich, and so on. Log entries might be as short as fifty words or just a block of code, but could be a few thousand words. They'll probably have source blocks, and might have images, but probably not - and those almost certainly won't be necessary to the content, and if it is, well, oh well.
****** Planning to Serialize Questions
Tangent to this, but I'd love love a way to serialize questions like this. I guess give each one a sequential ID, and have that be the lookup method?
- ID
  - Question
    - Feedback, the first piece would probably be an explanation.
    - More feedback, recorded answers.

So the functions:
- Add a new question.
- Return a list of IDs whose question contains the input word/phrase.
- Add a piece of text as feedback for a question. Preferably whatever the marked region is, seems easy enough, I just don't know how.

And of course functions to remove stuff, and helper functions - take a list of IDs, give a list of those questions, for example.

****** Thoughts on Attempts to Reform Online Communication
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: thoughts-on-attempts-to-reform-online-communication
      :END:
There quite a few people talking in the /Fediverse/ about how we can reform online communication. Quite a few Western folk. I've got a question for them?

Why are you "building a better Internet" before looking at how marginalized populations in authoritarian oppressive regions have been using the Internet for the past decade? If you wanted to understand the real threats that online communications face, you'd look to any of the millions who've challenged those threats, rather than wanting unthreatened people to gather around to hypothesize as to what those threats might be.

Folk who have no real experience in activism, yet are trying to place themselves in the vanguard of better communication technology. Interested in setting up new projects, new names, bringing together folks around them and their discussions, completely ignoring the work that's been done by many others in the field, because... ?

Honestly it seems like most folk are doing it because they truly think their opinion is that much better. I know this is conceited but part of it is personally offensive because like, I'm not allowed to leave the country, have basically been blacklisted from real jobs, because of my work in this field, and I watch these privileged (enough) folk saying "oh well it just needed a different protocol!" or something stupid, without recognizing... like this is real life.

If they're /actually/ making tools that remove the state's ability to control communication, they're like, gonna get shot, not sent emotionally draining DMs.

And so I think it kinda belays that they KNOW they aren't really doing these things, or that they don't know just how aggressive the government can be, and either way, demonstrates, they aren't qualified at all for what they're trying to do. Like, again, conceit, but tbh if you don't know my name, you aren't well-versed enough in the recent history of online activism to do more than be a peon or rabblerousers.

And it sucks because like with their "we're building better tools" that just... actively takes attention away from the folk in those oppressive regions actually doing that - and then people in those regions start using these shitty tools becuase they're what the marketed white folk use, and then folk start getting arrested or shot.

And, on top of this, I'm concerned these folk don't realize what they're getting into. Guards at Guantanamo aren't going to skip your beating that day because you're out of spoons, y'know?

****** Criticism of a Traveling Nerd
There's someone in the /Fediverse/ I follow who travels around Asia doing their tech job. Until now, I've always been impressed they hadn't seemed to have fallen to any of the propaganda around them.

I was wrong: this morning they were defending the neutrality of technical tools, saying technology should work for everyone, fascist and individual alike. They highlighted /Wikipedia/'s reputation for neutrality as a goal of the /Fediverse/. When I said /Wikipedia/ was mostly reputation, they asked for proof of editors changing pages for clear political purpose. That's like me saying "I think Sam likes pizza," and someone saying, "Until you show me Sam eating a pepperoni pizza from Leon's shop, I won't buy it."

What's weird is they truly seem to think that by demonstrating Wikipedia's edit pages aren't overtly political because of the accounts assigned to editors, it must be neutral. Which... is exactly the sort of unexamined faith in a reputation I was criticizing: it's hard to know if something is neutral, because omission can be highly guiding.

I also brought up that since Nuremberg, pointed centrism hasn't been considered a valid form of neutrality, and it wasn't even before then, so he's being disingenous, anachronistic, and all to argue that people who want to kill me should be able to use software I've made to do so.

Imagine thinking that's neutral!

Update; they've said that Wikipedia doesn't have an editor bias, and is less biased than newspapers. I'll agree with the latter but "less biased" isn't "neutral." They've also said they'll change their mind "in a blink," if the bias is demonstrated, but considering one of their other claims was resistance to fascists is equivalent to fascists, I don't know if i trust them to change their mind for the better.
****** Thoughts on Father Brown
I wrote less than I meant to yesterday, instead spending most of the day sleeping and watching /Father Brown/, a /BBC/ mystery drama with a touch of levity that often lands, but sometimes seems to be erasing some of the sins of the /Catholic Church/.

Which is shame because I appreciate how it does touch on some issues like post-War classism in England.

****** Org-mode Macros for Document Plating
      :PROPERTIES:
      :CUSTOM_ID: org-mode-macros-for-document-plating
      :END:
#+INDEX: Org-mode!macros!"Org-mode Macros for Document Plating"
In my documents there are certain paragraphs or sections that often get repeated: This document is copyright blah blah, for example.

I refer to these bits of text as "plates," partially short for "boilerplate," but mostly because, in printing, that's what they would've been, plates owned by the press, used across multiple runs of multiple books.

I implement a lot of my plates as /Org-mode macros/ that take arguments to call other macros. It's a bit convoluted but it's what I kind of stumbled naturally into, and works well enough for my purposes. (Though, now that I'm more familiar with Elisp, I'll be looking if there's ways I can improve them.)

At the moment I have two categories of plates, those for documents, and those for projects. I'll only be looking at the document ones right now. Here's a list of all the ones I have right now.

- =document-type=
  - This is a noun describing the form and function of a piece, something like "essay" or "CSS theme."
- =doc=
  - Simply a reference to =document-type=, a bit faster to type.
- =document-eli=
  - This macro takes an argument and calls another =document-eli-= macro - at the moment there's just =document-eli-default=. ELI is the document's "editorial and license information," a few sentences about its author, copyright, and any other important disclosures. (That's my own term to be clear, not something one should expect to know.)
- =document-eli-default=
  - My default ELI, which says the document is released, as best I can, into the public domain.
- =document-is-draft=
  - A sentence that warns the reader that the text that follows is incomplete.
- =document-has-project=
  - A sentence pointing to whatever project the document is a part of. This expects the document to have a fair number of other macros defined, so I think I might rework this one.
- =document-brief=
  - A summary of the document, mostly pulling in other macros.
- =document-implements-literate-programming=
  - A few sentences saying the document implements literate programming so will have an unusual presentation of source code. I should make another macro that warns readers about source code blocks, because it's unreasonable to expect them to have a familiarity with them prior to my text.
- =document-is-prototype=
  - This is a few sentences saying that the document is a prototype - usually this is for documents that are about software, to let readers know that the code won't be ready for use yet.
- =document-can-tangle=
  - This is a few sentences about how one can use thie file in /Emacs/ to extract the source code. I suppose this macro would only need to be included in =.org= format exports of the text.

As is evident, the macros tend to have a sort of sentence structure, subject-verb-object, where appropriate - I find this helps make it easier to skim past them when I'm proofing the document.

Some of the macros, like =document-brief=, are somewhat programmatic - in a way, they take a selection of inputs (document title, author, so on) and return an output. However, given that the "program" between the input and output is simply, "replace %1 with the first input, %2 with the second, and so on," it might not actually make any more sense to write them as functions instead of macros.

However, putting them inside functions would make it easier to pull in other information from /Emacs/ - for example, I might be able to pull in the document's wordcount.

And either way I can always put it back later, and this would give me a good opportunity to learn to work with /Org-mode/ data in /Elisp/.
******* Document Type Macros

A document type is a noun phrase about a document's form and function. It might be as general as "document," the global template behind =document-type= macro, or very specific, like "collection of layouts for the Hugo static site generator."

#+begin_src elisp
(setq org-export-global-macros
'(("document-type" . "document")
("doc" . "{{{document-type}}}")
("doc-type-function" . "(eval (ems-document-type))")))
#+end_src

With these two macros, I can say something like, =The {{{doc}}} is in early drafting stages.= and save myself a bit of typing.

I'm not sure what I'll use it for yet, but as I said, this is partially for practice: I'm going to write a function that returns the template for =document-type=.

If I understand the documentation properly, it should be equal to =(cdr (assoc "document-type" org-macro-templates))= - in other words, the value the key ="doc-type"= points to in the =org-macro-templates= alist, which is built by =org-macro-initialize-templates= at the start of the macro evaluation process. This assumes that the =doc-type= macr

 #+begin_src elisp
 (defun ems-document-type ()
 "This function returns a string containing a noun disclosing the type, based on form and function, of the document represented in the current Org-mode buffer."
 (let ((document-type (cdr (assoc "document-type" org-macro-templates))))
 document-type))
 #+end_src

In the future, I imagine I'll want to be able to optionally pass a buffer or file to the this function, and it can check that object's document-type instead.
******* Document Editorial and License Information Macros
This macro probably more properly requires a function to complete itself, but here's what it currently looks like:

#+begin_src elisp
(add-to-list 'org-export-global-macros
'("document-eli" . "{{{document-eli-$1}}}"))
(add-to-list 'org-export-global-macros
'("document-eli-default" . "This {{{document-type}}} was created by me, emsenn, in the United States. To the extent possible under law, I have waived all copyright and related or neighboring rights to this {{{document-type}}}. This {{{document-type}}} was created for the benefit of the public."))
#+end_src

I think I can reform this without deprecating my current API of ={{{document-eli(TYPE)}}}=. First I'll clear out those macros and set up a new one, just for =document-eli= that is set to the template =(eval (ems-document-eli "$1"))=.

#+begin_src elisp
(delete (assoc "document-eli" org-export-global-macros) org-export-global-macros)
(delete (assoc "document-eli-default" org-export-global-macros) org-export-global-macros)
(add-to-list 'org-export-global-macros
'("document-eli" . "(eval (ems-document-eli \"$1\"))"))
#+end_src

{{{document-eli(default)}}}

#+begin_src elisp
  (defun ems-document-eli (eli)
    "Returns a string about the editorial and licensing information of the open Org-mode buffer."
    (concat
     "This document was written by me, emsenn, in the United States."
     (if (string-equal eli 'default)
        "To the extent possible under law, I have waived all copyright and related or neighboring rights to this {{{doc}}}. This {{{doc}}} was created for the benefit of the public.")))
#+end_src

Not much different in effect, but I feel like this'll let me do more with it over time. And, as always, it's some practice; I didn't know the =string-equal= function before this, for example.

I've got to take a break, but later I'll pull in the other macros - should be fast enough, since they (except =document-brief=) probaby don't have anything programmatic to run with them.
****** Building a Hugo Theme for My Personal Website
#+INDEX: publishing!online!website generation!"Building a Hugo THeme for My Personal Website"
This section, that you're reading, lives under a section called "2019-06-25 Tuesday," which is in turn under a section called "2019-06 June," which is under a section called "2019," which is under a section called "Logs." At that same root level of the document, there is another section called "Compositions." Both the "Logs" and "Composition" sections have a property, =header-args=, which is set to, among other things, =:noweb t=, which lets me reference code blocks by name, inside other code blocks.

Under the "Compositions" section there is currently one subsection, "Hugo Personal Website Theme."

It contains, for now, the following bit of text:

#+begin_src org
  ,#+begin_src org :tangle ./comps/hugo-personal-website-theme.org
  <<hugo-personal-website-theme-introduction>>
  ,#+end_src
#+end_src

Then, when I tangle this document I'm writing in, it creates =./comps/hugo-personal-website-theme.org= and fills it with the source blocks pointed to by the =<< >>=.

I think this is how I'll handle writing longer documents while sticking to my (now 8-day long) challenge of doing all my work inside  Starfleet-style log. Getting sick over the weekend has certainly made that challenge easier, though.

So, my personal website. At the moment it is implemented across two compositions: my /Hugo personal website theme/ and my /emsenn's website/.

I've already pulled in the theme's introduction, now I'm going  too plan its actual contents.

So, five sections: an overview, operations (how to use the composition and resultant theme,) the theme's configuration, and then its various layouts and partial layouts: the implementation of the theme.

The overview is simple enough, just some introductory information:

#+name: hugo-personal-website-theme-overview
#+begin_src org
This {{{doc}}} provides resources for using /Hugo/, a /static site generator/.
#+end_src

The theme's configuration is pretty simple, but at this point, I need to take a step back.

#+name: hugo-personal-website-theme-configuration-intro
#+begin_src org
:PROPERTIES:
:header-args: :tangle ./theme.toml
:end:
Explanining the  configuration below, located at =./theme.toml=, is beyond the scope of this document. {{{source(hugo-theme)}}}.
#+end_src

There's a macro in there, =source=, that I haven't defined, but first, the configuration itself:

#+name: hugo-personal-website-theme-configuration
#+begin_src toml
name = "Hugo Personal Website Theme"
license = "CC0"
[author]
  name = "emsenn"
  homepage = "https://emsenn.net"
#+end_src
****** Defining a Macro for Linking to Term Sources
#+INDEX: Org-mode!macros!"Defining a Macro for Linking to Term Sources"
I've used an /Org-mode macro/ in my /Hugo personal website theme/, =source=, that's intended to expand out to a sentence linking to the argument's definition.

My personal dictionary works similar to how the /Elisp declarations/ for /Teraum/ work - or will. I've drafted them before, so am just going to write the code here without much explanation. Since =hugo-theme= is the term I've already used the =source= macro for, it's what I'll start with here.

#+begin_src elisp
(setq ems-dict
'(("hugo-theme"
(definition . "A collection of resources for Hugo, a static website generator, providing layouts for templating the site's content.")
(source . "https://gohugo.io/themes/"))))
#+end_src

A function to read back a term:

#+begin_src elisp
(defun ems-dict-read-term
(term)
"Returns the TERM from my dictionary."
(cdr (assoc term ems-dict)))
#+end_src


And a function to read out the definition...

#+begin_src elisp
(defun ems-dict-read-definition
(term)
"Return the definition of TERM from ems-dict."
(cdr (assoc 'definition (ems-dict-read-term term))))
#+end_src

And one to read out the source...

#+begin_src elisp
(defun ems-dict-read-source
(term)
"Return the source of TERM from ems-dict."
(cdr (assoc 'source (ems-dict-read-term term))))
#+end_src

And now, the macro that makes it a sentence:

#+begin_src elisp
  (add-to-list 'org-export-global-macros
  '("source" . "(eval (concat \"See [[\"(ems-dict-read-source \"$1\") \"]].\"))"))
#+end_src

All done! An aside, about this "writing code by logging," thing I'm doing. It leads to quite fragmented logs, but, I'm enjoying being able to just putz about.

   '("The CSS Mindset"
     (author . "Max Böck") (source . "https://mxb.dev/blog/the-css-mindset/"))
   (cdr (assoc 'pending ems-reading-list)))

****** Collecting Log Entries as Compositions

I've been using a single /Org-mode/ file as my main way of doing things on a computer for about a week now, accounting for the days I was sick. When I say "doing things," I mean everything from writing my journal to writing code for my Emacs client, to... well, that's it for now, I had a slow week and there's a lot that I'm not doing in this file because it needs to be done, and I can't take the time to figure out how to do everything in my life in this file at once, /especially/ because I'm not sure if this is how I'll want to do things long term. Though, I must say, for now, I'm quite enjoying it.

A bit ago, today, I started to work on my personal website's theme as a composition, but after taking a break, I realize I was doing a poor job of it and have since reapproached the problem. Unfortunately, the composition is handled outside of the logging now, so I can't explicitly show here what it is, but basically, under the "Compositions" section, there's the "Hugo Personal Website Theme" section, and instead of containing a codeblock which using Noweb to pull in the appropriate source blocks, it contains series of =#+INCLUDE= statements that pull in the whole relevant entry.
****** Brutstrap CSS Overview
#+INDEX: CSS!"Brutstrap CSS Overview"
*/Brutstrap/* is my CSS theme based on David Bryant Copeland's [[https://brutalist-web.design/]["Guidelines for Brutalist Web Design,"]] which advocates "/raw content true to its construction/."
******* Principles
- */The website people see is the website in the HTML file./* As David Byant Copeland says in his definition, brutalist websites should be "raw content, true to [their] construction." Here, that means the content is represented as a single column, flowing left to right and top to bottom.
- */Only links, inputs and buttons should respond to interaction./* A website should allow two points of interaction: a hyperlink that brings you to a destination, and form which submits information to a server. Those elements, and only those elements, should respond to being clicked, focused, or hovered-over.
- */Components should be named what they are./* Classes should be named what they are, not what they're for.
- */Design should be helpful./* Styling should be to help reinforce the context that your content has.
******* Implementation
   :PROPERTIES:
   :header-args: :tangle ./src/brutstrap.css :padline no
   :END:
*/NOTE:/* Because I use Org-mode, this CSS is written with rules
compatible with its naming scheme.
******** Document Framing
********* Body
#+name: brutstrap-body
#+BEGIN_SRC css
  body {
    position: relative;
    background-color: #eee;
    color: #444;
    font-family: serif;
    margin: 0 auto;
    padding-bottom: 6rem;
    min-height: 100%;
    font-size: 1.2em;
  }
#+END_SRC
********* Header & Title
#+name: brutstrap-header
#+BEGIN_SRC css
header, h1 {
  font-family: sans-serif;
  text-align: center;
  width: 100%;
  overflow: hidden;
  font-family: sans-serif;
}
.title { font-size: 3.2rem; }
.subtitle { font-size: 2.2rem; }
#+END_SRC
********* Content Container
#+name: brutstrap-content
#+BEGIN_SRC css
main, #content {
  width: 75vw;
  max-width: 40em;
  margin: 0 0 0 2em;
  line-height: 1.6;
  margin-bottom: 8rem;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Footer
#+name: brutstrap-footer
#+BEGIN_SRC css
footer, #postamble {
  padding: 1em 0;
  position: absolute;
  right: 0;
  bottom: 0;
  left: 0;
}
#+END_SRC
******** Block Design
********* Section
#+name: brutstrap-section
#+BEGIN_SRC css
section {
  border-bottom: 0.1em solid #444;
  margin-bottom: 1em;
}
.outline-3 {
  border-bottom: 0.1rem dotted #444;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Blockquote
#+name: brutstrap-blockquote
#+BEGIN_SRC css
blockquote {
  padding: 0.5rem;
  border-left: 0.1em solid #444;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Table
#+name: brutstrap-table
#+BEGIN_SRC css
table {
    border-collapse: collapse;
    border-spacing: 0;
    empty-cells: show;
    border: 0.1em solid #444;
}
thead { font-family: sans-serif; }
td {
  padding: 0 0.3em;
  border: 0.01em solid #444;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Images
#+name: brutstrap-images
#+BEGIN_SRC css
img {
  max-width: 80vw;
  vertical-align: middle;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Code
#+name: brutstrap-code
#+BEGIN_SRC css
pre, .src {
  padding: 0.5em;
  border: 0.1em solid #444;
  white-space: pre-wrap;
  overflow-x: scroll;
  text-overflow: clip;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Dictionary List
#+name: brutstrap-dictionary-list
#+begin_src css
dt {
  font-weight: normal;
  display: inline-block;
  border-bottom: 0.1rem dotted #444;
}
#text-index dt { border-bottom: none; }
#+end_src
******** Typography
********* Headlines
#+name: brutstrap-headlines
#+BEGIN_SRC css
h2, h3, h4, h5, h6, h7, h8, h9, h10 {
  display: block;
  font-family: sans-serif;
  margin: 0.5em;
}
h2 {
  text-align: center;
}
h2 { font-size: 2em; }
h3 {
  font-size: 1.8em;
}
h4 {
  font-size: 1.6em;
}
h5 {
  font-size: 1.4em;
  margin: 0.5em 0 0.2em 10%;
}
h6 {
  font-size: 1.2em;
  margin: 0.5em 0 0.2em 30%;
}
#+END_SRC
********* Links
#+name: brutstrap-links
#+BEGIN_SRC css
a {
  text-decoration: none;
  color: #3ac;
  display: inline;
  position: relative;
  border-bottom: 0.1rem dotted;
  line-height: 1.2;
  transition: border 0.3s;
}
a:hover {
  color: #5ce;
  outline-style: none;
  border-bottom: 0.1rem solid;
}
a:visited { color: #b8c; }
a:visited:hover { color: #dae; }
a:focus {
  outline-style: none;
  border-bottom: 0.1rem solid;
}
#+END_SRC
******** State Rules
********* Selection Rules
#+name: brutstrap-selection
#+BEGIN_SRC css
::selection { background-color: #777; color: #eee; }
a::selection { background-color: #ccc; }
#+END_SRC
******** Class Rules
********* TODO
#+name: brutstrap-todo
#+begin_src css
.todo {
  background-color: #ddd;
  color: #333;
  font-size: .8rem;
  float: right;
}
#+end_src
********* Example under Source
#+name: brutstrap-example-under-source
#+begin_src css
.org-src-container + .example {
  margin-left: 8em;
}
#+end_src
********* Listing Captions
#+name: brutstrap-listing-captions
#+begin_src css
label.org-src-name {
  margin: 0 0 0 3em;
  font-family: sans-serif;
  font-size: 1rem;
  line-height: 0;
}
#+end_src
****** Brutstrap CSS for Inlining with Org-Mode
This code block tangles out to =./brutstrap.inc=, to be used like =#+SETUPFILE: ./brutstrap.inc=.
