****** DRAFTING Team Qualification Procedure                     :@procedure:
     :PROPERTIES:
     :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: team-qualification-procedure
     :EXPORT_DATE: 2018-11-02
     :type: procedure
     :END:
If I'm considering joining a team, or work-group: any small group of people who are
expected to work as peers to accomplish one or more goals, I want to know what sort of
team it is.  I have developed the following procedure to for getting some information to
predict that.
******* Methodology
I provide a copy of the form below to all team-members, but not the leader(s) of the
team, with instructions to mark each statement which is true.  Results are collectedly
/blindly/: in a way that prevents me from knowing who provided which form.
******* Form for Team Members
- [0/12] Statements Marked
  - [ ] I am given clear expectations.
  - [ ] I understand how my work fits into the larger operation.
  - [ ] I am provided with the resources I find necessary.
  - [ ] I have opportunities to use my best skills.
  - [ ] A team member or lead has given recognition to my contributions in the last week.
  - [ ] A team member or lead has encouraged my personal development recently.
  - [ ] A team lead has encouraged my personal development in the last quarter.
  - [ ] My work for the team has led to the development of new skills.
  - [ ] Other team members contribute high-quality work.
  - [ ] My opinions are given due consideration.
  - [ ] A team member or lead has recognized my humanity in the past week.
  - [ ] I consider someone within the team a friend.
******* Interpreting Responses
It's desired that every statement be marked, but they're arranged in order of my
priority.  I care most that:
- My role and its meaning for the organization are clear.
- I won't lack resources. /(This would be more important, but I've found it possible to
  fix this problem from within a team... sometimes.)/
- Working with the team will help advance my skills.
- Team members think others on the team are doing good work.
- The team will make me feel like a respected human.
******* Conclusion
Once I've collected the results and interpreted them, I schedule a meeting with the team
leader(s) to ask any questions I might have.  Almost always, I at least ask, "is it
possibly secure more or different resources for the team," but depending on the results I
may ask:
- What project management methodology are you using?
- What expectations for communications and reporting have you established?

****** DRAFTING Contact Record Management Procedure
     :PROPERTIES:
     :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: contact-record-management-procedure
     :END:
This {{{section}}} contains my procedure for managing a */contact record/*: a collection
of data about a person or organization.

Most contact records are made for some special purpose, like to track those who have
opted-into receiving email newsletters, or have expressed interest in one of my services.
The procedures for that sort of work specify those records' deviations from this base-line
record.

Things a contact record should enable:
- Respectful communication with the contact
- A way for the contact to terminate communications

Here are the specific points of data I use in a contact record.
- */Preferred name/*:
- */Legal name/*:
- */Birth date/*:
- */Contact phone/*:
- */Contact email/*:
Here's an example contact record:
#+BEGIN_SRC org
  ,* emsenn
    :PROPERTIES:
    :PREFERRED_NAME: emsenn
    :LEGAL_NAME: Morgan Sennhauser
    :BIRTH_DATE: <1990-05-11>
    :CONTACT_PHONE: 814-367-3660
    :CONTACT_EMAIL: emsenn@emsenn.net
    :END:
#+END_SRC

This {{{section}}} contains my procedures for managing an */action/*: a set of things to
be done to accomplish an */objective/*: a qualified measure of a goal.  Put another way,
this {{{section}}} outlines how I do /task management/.

I've written this {{{section}}} for a professional audience, and described it as
implemented using [Org-mode](https://orgmode.org) for [Emacs](https://gnu.org/s/emacs/).
However, it works for personal actions as well, and can be implemented in a variety of
different softwares.  If you do so, I'd love to [hear about it](mailto:emsen@emsenn.net).

This {{{section}}} is part of my [Manual of Business](/manual-of-business), which is an
ever-growing collection of procedures like this one, about all facets of business
operations.
******* Introduction
My /action management procedures/ have developed organically since my professional life
started in the late 2000s, shaped by the whatever I was working on at that time.  It's
only in 2018 that I've begun begun documenting it as its own unique procedure, to develop
my portfolio and skills as a business operations consultant.

The procedures I've developed come from a lot of different /needs/ that I've felt when
working with different organizations.  These needs led to me making some decisions about
how I would, if I were in charge, handle the business of "doing work to meet an
objective."  Some of these decisions have been recorded as my [personal
directives](/personal-directives), which explain in detail my motivation behind the
decision.

It's taken me a long time to figure out how

****** DRAFTING Real Time Chat Service Operations               :@procedures:
     :PROPERTIES:
     :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: real-time-chat-service-operations
     :END:
This document is my guide for the creation and management of an organization's /real-time
chat service/, such as [Zulip]() or [Slack]().
******* Concepts
- */Administrators/*...
- */Direct/* (or /private/) */messages/* are messages sent to a single or several users,
  outside an organization's existing /streams/.
- */Moderators/*...
- */Public/* is a somewhat fluid concept, but generally means "not in /direct-message/."
- */Streams/*, or /channels/, are for sending and receiving messages among a large group
  of people.
- */Topics/*...
- */Users/* are people who have accounts on the chat service. In almost every service,
  you're able to /direct/ (or /private/) /message/ one or more other users.
- */Useful conversation/* is discussion that has a purpose. Anything involving
  decision-making, or questions about tech or client data, are useful
  conversations. Useful conversations should be /public/.
******* Service Management
********  Onboarding
******* Organizing Communication
One of the reasons employees like real-time chat services is because they view it as
/easy/. What they mean, in practice, is that it's easy to get into chat and start up a
conversation, send a notification, or just generally /talk/, in a way that's more informal
than email or other means. Service administrators can encourage this culture by making
good use of available features.
******** Naming Streams (or Channels)
Having guidelines for naming streams helps make it easier for people to make new streams
when they need them. For most organizations, it's appropriate to have:
- A stream for each department (~finance~, ~marketing~, ~sales~). /(You may also want a
  channel for departments that often help employees from across the company, such as
  ~finance-help~.)/
- A stream for each office location (~san-fransisco~, ~utopia-planitia~)

It may also be beneficial to have streams based on:
- Cross-department groups (~website-group~)
- Feature or project development (~mobileapp-project~)

Stylistically, stream names should be:
- lowercase
- hyphenated-between-words
- begin with the specific, end with the category
******** Naming Topics
/Topics/ are a way of organizing conversations within streams available within Zulip. I
would recommend naming them similarly to [issues](/gitlab/issues/).
******* Moderation
******** As a User
Many chat services allow for dedicated /moderators/. However, it may be appropriate for
normal users to moderate conversations they're participating in:
- Remind people that /useful conversation/ should be /public/.

This {{{ section }}} is my attempt to explain what the
***** DRAFTING Product Design Procedures                              :ALPHA:
****** Procedures for Establishing a New Product
******* Procedure for Designing a New Product
******* Procedure for Establishing a Product Cost and Pricing
******* Procedure for Establishing a Product Revenue Goal
******* Procedure for Measuring the Necessary Number of Visitors
      :PROPERTIES:
      :liaison:  emsenn
      :Effort:   0:10
      :END:
 After you have determined your product's price \( p \) and revenue goal \( R \), you must
 determine how many */visitors/* the product needs to receive in a period to meet that
 goal, assuming minimal success.

 By minimal success, we */assume a 3.15% conversion rate/* for visitors-to-leads and
 leads-to-sales.  (Considering known industry rates of 5% for websites and 8% for physical
 retail, this is assuming /very/ minimal success.)  This leads to the formula
 \( (((R/p)/0.0315)/0.0315)\=v \), where ~R~ is your revenue goal, ~p~ is your product price,
 and ~v~ is how many visitors you need.

 This can be rewritten, losing a little precision, as \( ((R*1000))/p)=v) \): revenue goal
 times 1000, divided by price, equals how many visitors you need.  That formula again is:

 \begin{equation}
 ( ( R * 1000 / p ) = v
 \end{equation}
******* Measuring Market Size
******* Comparing Market Size and Product Type to Estimate Market Awareness
******* Comparing Market Awareness with Necessary Number of Visitors

****** New Staff Training
******* Summary
  This section includes the lessons for new employees of /Gill and Sennhauser/.  They will
  explain the company's values and principles, and how to ensure that you, the new employee,
  can fit in with the organization.

  These modules are not about benefits or how to set up your computer.  They're about the
  type of work you'll be doing at /Gill and Sennhauser/, the decisions you'll make, and how
  you should think about them.
******* New Staff Training, Module 1: Your First Day
  You've been interviewed, you've signed contracts You know a few people's names, and you're
  ready to go through the onboarding.

  /Gill and Sennhauser/ is, for a medley of reasons, uses procedures and an organization
  that may be different than what you're used to.  If any part of what this and subsequent
  lessons explain is unclear, please reach out and let someone know.

  The first thing you need to know is: *we don't expect you to be productive your first few
  days* - if not a week!  Instead, you're expected to spent the time learning about the
  company, getting to know people, and going through your onboarding.

  Before anything else, here are some important facts you should know about /Gill and
  Sennhauser/.  You may have already heard all or some of these during the hiring process,
  but they're all worth refreshing.
******** New Staff Training, Module 1, Lesson 1: Company Values
  - *We're self-funded.* We have not - and are not interested in - bringing in outside
    financing.  This gives us the freedom to define the company's objectives and methods
    however we see best.
  - *We support open-source.* We support open-source - both using open-source tools, and
    releasing what we can as public resources ourselves.
  - *We have a public list of objectives.* It's over there.  We make sure everyone on the
    staff is familiar with them, so that they know how what they're working on relates back
    to those objectives.
  - *We don't have a staff hierarchy.* Everyone at /Gill and Sennhauser/ is a partner.  This
    doesn't mean everyone is compensated equally, but it does mean we don't have managers or
    employees.  Everyone has a role they fill, but even that is fairly fluid.  We believe
    this lets us get the most out of our staff, while giving them the most we can. /(After
    all, it is just a job.)/ This structure removes the organizational barrier between you
    and your work, making you directly responsible for helping /Gill and Sennhauser/ reach
    its objectives.
******** New Staff Training, Module 1, Lesson 2: Company Lingo
******** New Staff Training, Module 1, Lesson 3: Your Staff Record
  When we decided to hire you, someone - probably whoever was responsible for your
  onboarding - made a */staff record/* for you: an entry in the /Gill and Sennhauser/ main
  record about you - well, you as a member of the staff; we try not to keep too much
  unnecessary personal info on anyone, even our staff.

  You should go ahead and pull up that record alongside this lesson.

  Next, we'll go over how to understand your staff record |TK|

  Now that you're a member of the staff, we'll expect you to maintain your own staff record
  (though others may make their own edits.)  Here are some situations when you'll need to
  update your staff record |TK|

  It's also recommended you add a habit to your agenda, for taking the time to verify the
  accuracy of your staff record.  Here's an example: |TK|
******* New Staff Training, Module 2: Your First Month
  You've finished your onboarding tasks.  You can navigate Emacs like a professional... who
  retired several decades ago.  And most importantly, you're ready to get started working.
  This lesson explains how you should figure out what to work on.

  At /Gill and Sennhauser/, work is divided into */activities/*: actionable records of
  something to be done.  Every activity relates back to a /target/, which in turn relates
  back to an /objective/.  Even as a member of the staff, it's your own responsibility to
  figure out how to figure out what activities you should be working on, introducing
  yourself to the people fulfilling the activities' other roles, and of course, getting the
  work done.
******** New Staff Training, Module 2, Lesson 1: Finding Activities
  Finding something to work on can be as simple as looking at the
   something that looks interesting, looking at who is responsible for
  the activity, and asking how you can get involved.

  We recognize that can be overwhelming, especially at first, so this lesson will help give
  you some criteria for deciding what to work on.

******** New Staff Training, Module 2, Lesson 2: Entering a Role

This {{{section}}} contains my procedures for managing */leads/*: entities (people or
organizations) who are potentially qualified buyers of a product, and have shared at least
one piece of contact information with me.

These procedures are in /early development/, though I've worked alongside sales structures
at multiple organizations, and currently manage the marketing and sales of a small
apartment building.

**** DRAFTING Manual of Writing Procedures                            :ALPHA:
   :PROPERTIES:
   :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: manual-of-writing
   :creationDate: <2018-07-31 Tue>
   :END:
This document outlines how I use a ticketing system, version control, and a static site generator to plan, draft, edit, and publish written works.

I've combined various open-source software development methods and tools to compromise between two perspectives I have:
- As a _**writer**_, I want to write when I'm able, and deal with everything else... later.
- As a _**developer**_, I want to avoid all the hardship my writer perspective causes. I don't want to spend time collecting notes, and I don't want to have to do a bunch of extra work to get my finished draft into publishing shape.

_**Table of Contents**_
- [Step-by-Step](#step-by-step)
- [Tools](#tools)
- [Concepts](#concepts)
  - [Issues](#issues)
  - [Branches and Commits](#branches-and-commits)
  - [Front-matter and Markdown](#front-matter-and-markdown)
  - [Deploying](#deploying)

### Step-by-Step
- When I have an idea for something to write about, I use an online form, or send an email to a special address.
- When I want to write about it, I go to a webpage that got made based on the form or email. The web page kind of like a social media post, just for that idea: it has comment threads, and other helpful stuff I'll cover later. This is where I talk through the idea, come up with an outline, and so on.
- When I'm ready to create a my rough draft, I go back to that webpage and click a big green button. This creates a new copy of my writing portfolio for me to draft in. I can download this copy, but normally I just use an online text-editor.
- When I'm finished with my draft, I merge its copy back in with my main portfolio, and it automatically gets "published" to my website.

### Tools
There are four tools I use to implement this process:
- _**Git**_ is my change-tracking tool, which records my portfolio's contents and history in a _**repository**_ and helps me keep track of different copies.
- _**Gitlab.com**_ is a website that offers services to help manage Git repositories.
- _**Markdown**_ is a way to style text by writing the formatting like punctution. It `_looks_ like **this**` and _turns_ into **this**.
- _**Gutenberg**_ is my main publishing tool, which takes my Markdown drafts and turns them into pretty HTML documents.
- _**Netlify**_ is a platform for deploying web applications, and is what I use to publish the HTML documents Gutenberg creates as my website.

### Concepts
#### Issues
Every piece I write starts as an **issue** associated with my a copy of my Git repository hosted on Gitlab.com. Gitlab defines _issues_ as: "a complete tool for tracking the evolution of a new idea."

Issues have two main components:
- A _**title**_. This should be fairly short and specific - normally I just use the piece's potential title in quotes.
- A _**description**_. An issue's description is a long text field that I can come back and edit.

They also have other attributes to help with managing them such as labels, comment threads, and due dates.

Usually, I create new issues by going to the appropriate webpage and filling out a form, but it's also possible for me to send an email.

If I send an email, the email's subject and body get turned into the title and description. If I use the online form, there's fields for both - as well as other attributes an issue may have like labels or due dates.

When it's created, a new issue is also assigned a number, which is used in many ways to quickly reference the issue.

_(I've written separate documentation for how I use [description templates](/guides/gitlab/description-templates/) and [labels](/guides/gitlab/labels/) to make creating and managing issues easier. You can use Gitlab to view my actual [description template for `new-content`](https://gitlab.com/emsenn/personal/raw/master/.gitlab/issue_templates/new-content.md) and [labels](https://gitlab.com/emsenn/personal/labels).)_

#### Branches and Commits
When I'm ready to begin a new draft, I go to the relevant issue. Underneath the description, but above the comments, is a button to _Create a new branch_.

Within the Git repository that is my portfolio, there are several _**branches**_: diverging versions of the repository's history and contents. The main branch is called the `master` branch, and the files in that branch are automatically turned into my website by Netlify. When I _Create a new branch_, Gitlab makes a new copy of my portfolio - a new branch - named after the issue.

For example: My idea is to write an article about how the TV show Galavant is really great. So, I make an issue titled `"Galavant is Great"` by filling out the web form. The new issue is given a number, `#60`. On the issue's page, I press _Create a new branch_, which creates a new branch, `60-galavant-is-great`: named after the issue's number and title.

I can access this new branch a variety of ways. Often, I just use Gitlab.com's text-editor, but there's also tools for working with git repositories locally.

In addition to branches, Git adds another important feature to my portfolio: **commits**.

In addition to saving a file to the hard-drive, Git requires you to _commit_ those changes to the repository, along with a brief note about the changes. Each commit has a record of what's different about it, compared to the previous commit. This builds up a really robust history of my portfolio, telling me not just when a file was made or changed, but what lines were changed and _why_.

When I'm done working on my draft and am ready to publish it, I _**merge**_ the draft's branch in with `master` branch. This creates a new commit, where the changes are whatever is different between the `master` branch and the draft branch - usually just the draft itself.

#### Front-matter & Markdown
As I've said, I write my documents in Markdown. To help them work with Gutenberg, the tool that turns them into a website, each document includes **front-matter**.

_Front-matter_ includes stuff like the document's title and description, publication date, copyright, and author. I've also developed other things to put in the front-matter, that Gutenberg takes into account when rendering the document.

Here's what my draft might look like on my example article:

```markdown
+++
title = "Galavant is Great"
description = "An essay about the TV show Galavant."
date = 2018-01-01

[extra]
author = "emsenn"
+++

_Galavant_ was a musical comedy that premiered on ABC in 2015...
```

_(I'm currently in the process of writing a [style-guide for my own writing](https://gitlab.com/emsenn/personal/issues/6), and an explanation of the [front-matter I use on my website](https://gitlab.com/emsenn/personal/issues/62).)_

#### Deployment
Every time I make a commit to any branch, Gitlab tells Netlify. Netlify grabs the new copy, and uses Gutenberg to create the website and then uploads that to its network of servers.

Where the website shows up depends on which branch the commit is on.
- Every commit is available `https://{{commit-hash}}--emsenn.netlify.com`. Commit hashes are long bits of gibberish that are unique identifiers for a specific commit.
  - Example: `https://5b5c15253813f019c6cd086e--emsenn.netlify.com`
- Every branch's most recent commit is available at `https://{{branch-name}}--emsenn.netlify.com`
  - Example: `https://60-galavant-is-great--emsenn.netlify.com`
- The `master` branch's most recent commit is available at `https://emsenn.net`.

At the moment I don't use the commit links, but the branch links are handy for giving to to folk when I'm looking for feedback. I'm fortunate to have a group of friends who seem perpetually eager to criticize me, and I'm happy to supply them with material.

---

I encourage you to look at my [`personal` repository](https://gitlab.com/emsenn/personal/), where this system is implemented.

{{ subscriptionform(cta="If you found this interesting or even useful, you might enjoy my email list.") }}

**** DRAFTING Manual of Publishing Procedures                       :@manual:
***** DRAFTING Procedure for Styling a Document with a Color Theme :@procedure:publishing:
    :PROPERTIES:
    :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedure-for-styling-a-document-with-a-color-theme
    :END:
This {{{section}}} is an explanation of how to build a /color styling/ for a document by
using /color attribute parameters/ consisting of at least a /primary hue/, but optionally
/saturation/, /light/, and a /secondary/ set of /color parameters/.  (This grouping of
/color attribute parameters/ is referred to as a /color theme/.)

The first section below, */[Themes](#themes)/*, further specifies that definition of a /color
theme/.

The second section below, */[Color Definitions](#color-definitions)/*, documents how the
specific colors to be used in the /color styling/ are generated from the /color theme/.
The specific colors are:
- */[Primary Color](#primary-color)/*, which is used in patterns and lines in the
  document.
- */[Secondary Color](#secondary-color)/*, which is used to fill white space.
- */[Background Color](#background-color)/*, which is used behind text.
- */[Font Color](#font-color)/*, which is used to color the text.
- */[Background Contrast Color](#background-contrast-color)/*, which is used to highlight
  text against the background.
- */[Font Contrast Color](#font-contrast-color)/*, which is used to color text within an
  element using the /background contrast color/.

The third section below, */[Elements](#elements)/*, specifies which colors should be applied
to which parts of a document.  The colored elements are:
- */[Primary Pattern Fill](#primary-pattern-fill)/*, the line color of the background
  pattern in HTML documents
- */[Background](#background)/*, the color of the document's margins
- */[Content Background](#content-background)/*, the color of the background of the
  document's content.
****** Themes
A */color theme/* is a set of parameters representing up to two sets of */color attribute parameters/*: a set of
hue, saturation, and light.  /(It may be useful to read the [Wikipedia article on the HSL
color model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSL_and_HSV).)/

The only required parameter is /primary hue/ (~primaryHue~): this defines where the
primary color value is in a circular red-green-blue color space.  That is, a hue of 0 or
360 is red, 120 is green, and 240 is blue.

The /primary saturation/ parameter defines the colorfulness of the primary color parameter,
and is a measure of percent.  The /primary light/ parameter defines the tint or shade of
the primary color value, and is a measure of percent with 0 being shaded pure black and
100 being tinted pure white.

The [Color Definitions](#color-definitions) below come with conditions for assuming any
missing parameters.
****** Color Definitions
******* Primary Color
The */primary color/* is used in the /[primary pattern fill](#primary pattern-fill)/ element.
******** Primary Color Hue
The */primary color hue/* is defined by the color theme's /primary hue/.  If that is not
set, 200, a blue, is used as the default.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if primaryHue:
  Hue = primaryHue
else:
  Hue = 200
#+END_SRC
******** Primary Color Saturation
The */primary color saturation/* is defined by the color theme's /primary saturation/.  If
that is not set but /secondary saturation/ is and is greater than 60%, the /primary color
saturation/ is defined as ~80%~.  Otherwise, it is defined as ~60%~.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if primarySaturation:
  Saturation = primarySaturation
else if secondarySaturation:
  if secondarySaturation > 60:
    Saturation = 80
else:
  Saturation = 60
#+END_SRC
******** Primary Color Light
The */primary color light/* is defined by the color theme's /primary light/.  If that is not
set, but the /secondary light/ is, /primary color light/ is defined as the difference
between 100 and it.  Otherwise, /primary color light/ is defined as 20.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if primaryLight:
  Light = primaryLight
else if secondaryLight:
  Light = (100 - secondaryLight)
else:
  Light = 20
#+END_SRC
******* Secondary Color
The */secondary color/* is used in the /[background](#background)/ element.
******** Secondary Color Hue
The */secondary color hue/* is defined by the theme's /secondary hue/.  If that is not
set, but /primary hue/ is, it is used instead.  Otherwise, a default hue of 200, a blue,
is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if secondaryHue:
  Hue = secondaryHue
else if primaryHue:
  Hue = primaryHue
else:
  Hue = 200
#+END_SRC
******** Secondary Color Saturation
The */secondary color saturation/* is defined by the theme's /secondary saturation/.  If
that is not set, but /primary saturation/ is, that value is used unless it is greater than 50 in
which case the difference between it and 50 is subtracted from 50 and used.  Otherwise, a
value of 20 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if secondarySaturation:
  Saturation = secondarySaturation
else if primarySaturation:
  if primarySaturation > 50:
    Saturation = (50 - (primarySaturation - 50))
  else:
    Saturation = primarySaturation
else:
  Saturation = 20
#+END_SRC
******** Secondary Color Light
The */secondary color light/* is defined by the theme's /secondary light/.  If that is not
set, but /primary light/ is, the /secondary color light/ is defined as the difference
between 100 and it.  Otherwise, a value of 20 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC
if secondaryLight:
  Light = secondaryLight
else if primaryLight:
  Light = (100 - primaryLight)
else:
  Light = 80
#+END_SRC
******* Background Color
The */background color/* is used for the /[content background](#content-background)/ element.
******** Background Color Hue
The */background color hue/* is defined by the theme's /secondary hue/.  If that is not
provided, but /primary hue/ is, that is used.  Otherwise, a value of 200 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if secondaryHue:
  Hue = secondaryHue
else if primaryHue:
  Hue = primaryHue
else:
  Hue = 200
#+END_SRC
******** Background Color Saturation
The */background color saturation/* is defined by the theme's /secondary saturation/.  If
that is not provided, but the /primary saturation/ is, that is used.  Otherwise, a value
of 20 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if secondarySaturation:
  Saturation = secondarySaturation
else if primarySaturation:
  Saturation = primarySaturation
else:
  Saturation = 20
#+END_SRC
******** Background Color Light
The */background color light/* is defined as the theme's /secondary light/ divided by 10
added to 90.  If the /secondary light/ is not provided, the /primary light/ is used
instead.  Otherwise, a value of 98 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if secondaryLight:
  Light = ((secondaryLight / 10) + 90)
else if primaryLight:
  Light = ((primaryLight / 10) + 90)
else:
  Light = 90
#+END_SRC
******* DRAFTING Font Color
The */font color/* is used for the /[content text](#content-text)/ element.
******** DRAFTING Font Color Hue
The */font color hue/* is defined by the theme's /primary hue/.  If that is not provided,
a default of 200 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if primaryHue:
  Hue = primaryHue
else:
  Hue = 200
#+END_SRC
******** DRAFTING Font Color Saturation
The */font color saturation/* is defined as the theme's /primary saturation/ divided by 5
added to 20.  If that is not provided, a default of 24 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if primarySaturation:
  Saturation = ((primarySaturation / 5) + 20)
else:
  Saturation = 24
#+END_SRC
******** DRAFTING Font Color Light
The */font color light/* is defined as the theme's /primary light/ divided by 10 added
to 15.  If that is not provided, a default of 18 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if primaryLight:
  Light = ((primaryLight / 10) + 15)
else:
  Light = 18
#+END_SRC
******* DRAFTING Background Contrast Color
The */background contrast color/* is used for the /[selection](#selection)/ and
/[focused](#focused)/ elements.
******** DRAFTING Background Contrast Hue
The */background contrast hue/* is defined by the theme's /primary hue/.  If that is not
provided, a default of 200 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if primaryHue:
  Hue = primaryHue
else:
  Hue = 200
#+END_SRC
******** DRAFTING Background Contrast Saturation
The */background contrast saturation/* is defined as the theme's /primary saturation/,
unless that parameter is less than fifty, in which case the parameter is added to 50 and
used.  If /primary saturation/ is not set, a default value of 70 is used.
#+BEGIN_SRC python
if primarySaturation:
  if primarySaturation < 50:
    Saturation = (primarySaturation + 50)
else:
  Saturation = 70
#+END_SRC
******** DRAFTING Background Contrast Light
The */background contrast light/* is defined as the theme's...
****** Element Coloring
******* Primary Pattern Fill
The */primary pattern fill/* is defined as the /[primary color](#primary-color)/, and is
used to color the lines in the pattern used in the document's background.
******* Page Background
The */page background/* is defined as the /[secondary color](#secondary-color)/.

In HTML documents, it is used as the color for the ~background-color~ rule in the ~<body>~
tag.
******* Content Background
The */content background/* is defined as the /[background color](#background-color)/.

In HTML documents, it is used as the color for the ~background-color~ rule in the
~<header>~, ~<main>~, and ~<footer>~ tags.
******* Content Text
******* Selection
******* Focused
***** Appendices to the Manual of Publishing Procedures
******* List of Color Themes
This {{{section}}} contains a list of color themes I have created for [applying a colorful
style to a document](#styling-a-document-with-a-color-theme).
******** Albion
| PARAMETER            | SETTING |
| primary hue          |     210 |
| primary saturation   |      60 |
| primary light        |      85 |
| secondary hue        |      47 |
| secondary saturation |      40 |
| secondary light      |      88 |
******** Blueprint
| PARAMETER            | SETTING |
| primary hue          |     240  |
| primary saturation   |      90 |
| primary light        |      70 |
| secondary hue        |      64 |
| secondary saturation |      12 |
| secondary light      |      90 |
**** DRAFTING Manual of Style                                       :@manual:
   :PROPERTIES:
   :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: manual-of-style
   :END:
This {{{section}}} contains my manual of */styling/* writing: constructing it in a way
that I think most accurately and precisely communicative.

It is in /early/ development.
***** The Written Voice
This section contains a general description of the /voice/ I try to
use.  By /voice/, I mean the mix of content and style that gives
my writing its personality.  I wrote every rule in
this manual with the primary objective of clarifying my voice.

My /tone/ might change depending on what I'm writing, but my voice should stay consistent.
****** Write Respectfully
An important part of my tone is */writing respectively/*: making positive assumptions
about the writing's subject and audience.

To treat the reader with respect:
- I don't tell them how to feel.

To treat the subject with respect I:
- I acknowledge people have qualities, but are not those qualities. (For details, see
  [Describing a Person](#describing-a-person)).
***** The Rules of Writing
The "rules" of writing are those relatively immutable standards which guide the
construction of a clause or sentence.  I call them rules because that's what everyone else
seems to call them.  Personally, I don't think they should be viewed that strictly.
****** Rules of Grammar
****** Rules of Punctuation
****** Rules of Spelling and the Treatment of Specific Words
******* Spelling
******** UK or US?
******* Noun Style Guide
******** Pronouns
********* Gender
 I hate that there needs to be this section but there does.
******** Proper Noun Style Guide
********* Specific Proper Noun Style Guide
  This section contains how to write specific proper nouns.  It
  is simply a list of proper nouns that /Gill and Sennhauser/
  has found itself writing several times, so wanted to establish
  a precise styling for.
  | Noun                | Notes           |
  | Gill and Sennhauser | no ampersand    |
  | Org-mode            | not "Mode"      |
  | emsenn              | not captialised |
******** Collective Noun Style Guide
| singular noun  | formal collective noun(s) | comedic collective noun(s) |
| congressperson | members of congress       | a swamp                    |
| yak            | cabinet, herd             | tangle, gridlock           |
| lawyer         | huddle, group             | tangle                     |
******* Verb Style Guide
******** Verb Specifications
********* "To," not "in order to," or "as a way to,"
********* "Also," not "in addition"
********* remove, not extract, eliminate, take away
   I mean, unless those are what make more sense.
********* use
   Normally, you should use "use" in your sentences, unless you're speaking of something that
   is being used in an unusual way, then you /can/ say utilize.  But you should probably
   still say use.
********* method or methodology
   Usually method, unless it's not.
******* Contraction Style Guide
  For my personal tone, I should:
  - Use common contractions:
    - it's
    - you're
    - that's
    - don't

  In all writing, I should:
  - Never mix contractions and their full form in the same piece.  This is bad: /If you
    don't secure the hatch, you cannot proceed with decompression./
  - Never form contractions from a noun and verb.  This is bad: /the client's almost here./
  Use common contractions, such as it’s, you’re, that's, and don’t, to create a friendly, informal tone.

  Don't mix contractions and their spelled-out equivalents in UI text. For example, don’t
  use can’t and cannot in the same UI.

  Never form a contraction from a noun and a verb, such as /emsenn’s developing a lot of
  new cloud services/.

  Avoid ambiguous contractions, such as there’d, it’ll, and they’ll.
******* Adverb Style Guide
******** Forbidden Adverbs
 Obviously not /forbidden/, but, avoid them.
 | Adverb        | Suggested alternative                                                                                 |
 | clearly       | Drop it.  If the sentence feels barren without it, maybe it's not that clear and you should rephrase. |
 | completely    | Whatever it did, it did it.  You only need to specify if it was incomplete.                           |
 | exceedingly   |                                                                                                       |
 | excellent     |                                                                                                       |
 | extremely     |                                                                                                       |
 | fairly        |                                                                                                       |
 | few           |                                                                                                       |
 | huge          |                                                                                                       |
 | interestingly | Leave it to your reader to decide if they're interested                                               |
 | largely       |                                                                                                       |
 | literally     |                                                                                                       |
 | many          |                                                                                                       |
 | mostly        |                                                                                                       |
 | quite         |                                                                                                       |
 | relatively    |                                                                                                       |
 | remarkably    |                                                                                                       |
 | several       |                                                                                                       |
 | signficantly  |                                                                                                       |
 | substantially |                                                                                                       |
 | surprisingly  |                                                                                                       |
 | tiny          |                                                                                                       |
 | various       |                                                                                                       |
 | vast          |                                                                                                       |
 | very          |                                                                                                       |
 Avoid vague adverbs:
 - clearly
 - completely
 - exceedingly
 - excellent
 - extremely
 - fairly
 - few
 - huge
 - interestingly
 - largely
 - literally
 - many
 - mostly
 - quite
 - relatively
 - remarkably
 - several
 - significantly
 - substantially
 - surprisingly
 - tiny
 - various
 - vast
 - very
******* Adjective Style Guide
******** Order of Adjectives
********* List of the Order of Adjectives
 Adjectives should occur be written in the following order:
 1) *Quantity:* many, seventeen, a few
 3) *Opinion:* interesting, good, gorgeous
 4) *Size:* big, narrow, tall
 5) *Quality:* broken, uncut, smooth
 6) *Shape:* square, rotund, triangular
 7) *Age:* 12-year-old, young, elderly
 8) *Color:* cerulean, green, pink
 9) *Origin:* English, Roman, Argentinian
 10) *Material:* Wood, aluminium, concrete
 11) *Type:* U-shaped, professional, engineer's
 12) *Purpose:* cleaning, camping, baking
********* Superseding Rules to the Order of Adjectives
 - If you've got something that might be a reduplicative /(hocus-pocus, mama)/, it might be
   worth changing the order to give the noun phrase a better meter.  The chief example of
   this is the /big bad/ wolf.
 - Modifiers to an adjective should directly precede them in the phrase.  This is most
   important for inserting a negation - that is, making sure to write, "there were several
   not calm individuals gathered at the gates." /(Note "not calm" would more properly be
   "excited," or a more appropriate positive adjective, but "not calm" is more suited for
   the piece.  This demonstrates that even when you violate some rules to achieve the
   proper voice and tone, you don't suddenly throw out the others.)
********* Examples from Literature
 - "It was cold, bleak, biting weather," in /A Christmas Carol/ by Charles Dickens
 - "He's an extraordinary looking man," in /The Strange Case of Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde/ by
   Robert Louis Stevenson
 - "And each separate dying ember wrought its ghost upon the floor," in /The Raven/ by
   Edgar Allan Poe
********* Examples from Our Portfolio
 Coming soon - just as soon as we have a portfolio!
******** Adjective Specifications

****** Guidelines for Determining Structure
 This section contains guidelines for determining how you should structure a piece of
 writing, for example a short list of software dependencies the reader will need to
 install.
***** Tone
*/Tone/* is that variable element of writing which conveys the piece's attitude and
intention.  Word choice, sentence and paragraph structure, layout, and other attributes
all factor into defining a pieces /tone/.

I find it most helpful to define a piece's tone with adjectives, such as /formal/,
/colloquial/, or /emotional/.  When creating a piece, I record these as properties, sorted
from highest to lowest importance.  That is, when two tone adjectives have contradicting
rules, the one written first is the one whose rules are followed.

******** Writing Flow, Lesson One: The Assignment
 This lesson /(is in early development and)/ will teach you about */assignments/*, the unit
 of work for the writing necessary to accomplish an objective.  Some examples of
 assignments are:
 - a bundle of pieces for a client.
 - a single piece written as a bid for client.
 - a series of emails for an internal marketing campaign.
********* Assignment Properties
	:PROPERTIES:
	:END:
 */Assignment properties/* are standardized pieces of information about an assignment.
 Assignment properties include things like the piece's length requirements and its tone.
 Assignment properties are located in three places, and are /inherited/ on-top of each other
 in this order:
 - First, the assignment should have whatever properties are in the Assignment Template.
 - Second, the assignment should have whatever properties are recommended by the
   appropriate style guide.
 - Finally, the assignment may include its own properties.

 Here's an example of what an assignment's properties might look like:

     #+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
     :PROPERTIES:
     :minimum-length: 700
     :recommended-length: 900
     :max-length: 1000
     :tone: sophisticated terse
     :END:
     #+END_EXAMPLE

 This section /(is in early development and)/ will teach you
 how to conduct research for a writing assignment.

******* Prerequisites
 This training assumes you have completed the following
 trainings:
 -Writing Flow Training
******* Instructions
******** High-End Hotel Description Writing, Lesson One: Interpretting the Assignment
 This lesson /(is in early development and)/ will teach you how
 to interpret an assignment.

 Assignments are kept in the Writing Assignments lesson's assignment is
 specifically.  You may notice the
 assignment is brief: most assignments will include relatively few guidelines, and will
 rely on your training and descretion as a writer.
******** High-End Hotel Description Writing, Lesson Two: Tone and Style
       :PROPERTIES:
       :END:
 The tone for this piece should be *sophisticated* while remaining *natural* and
 *authentic*. /(Remember to check the section for guidance on these
 terms.)/

 The reader should be addressed in *second-person plural*, to connect them to the hotel and
 "travel" through the article.  While it's appropriate to emphasize the luxury of a 5-star
 hotel, do not over-sell a 3-star hotel.

 The following should be applied over your */assignment specification/*:

     #+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
     :PROPERTIES:
     :tone: sophisticated natural authentic :perspective: third
     :address: second-person plural
     :END:
     #+END_EXAMPLE
******** High-End Hotel Description Writing, Lesson Three: Structure
 Expert opinion (15 words exactly), in the form of a quote,
 given in the hotel brief.  E.g. Tom Gill, London: “A
 contemporary hotel which provides tranquillity right in the
 heart of London's historical city centre.”

 We love (80 words) in a list form

 This highlights why the expert has chosen this offer,
 explaining what they loved about the hotel and the
 destination. There should be 4 or 5 ‘We Love’ points which are
 communicated in the hotel brief.

 Don’t hesitate to add personal touches to the points,
 e.g. complimentary breakfasts: give examples of what you can
 eat, where you can eat it.
******** High-End Hotel Description Writing, Advanced Lesson: Tom's Musings
 Think of some USPs (unique selling points) that help the hotel
 stand out. Maybe it's close to the beach or has a spa
 facility, the most important thing is drawing in the reader
 immediately. Don’t forget to mention the location of the
 establishment, the number of stars, the surrounding areas and
 the style/architecture of the place.

 Example: “In Saintes-Maries-de-la-Mer, where the Mediterranean
 Sea and the Rhône Delta meet, one can experience traditional
 atmosphere and spectacular surroundings. The 5-star hotel, Les
 Arnelles, is a charming building defined by a harmonious
 relationship with the great outdoors, both inside and out. Its
 balance of contemporary and natural aesthetics firmly roots
 the hotel as the standout place to stay.”
******** High-End Hotel Description Writing, Advanced Lesson: Room Descriptions
 For each room, write a title with the name of the room  included and the size in brackets. Don’t hesitate to reiterate  the type of person who could benefit from this room (family,  couple). Describe the benefits of the room: the decoration,
 the surrounding area, etc.

 Mention facilities (complimentary or optional), size, view,  style, who can stay (no. of adults/children, babies allowed or  not).

 Example: Beach Front Villas (95 m²): “Situated directly on the  beach, these villas are ideal for a trip with the whole  family; up to two adults and two children can comfortably stay here. You also have direct access to the beach and swimming  pool; putting paradise outside your hotel door."

***** Gitlab
****** Gitlab Description Templates
+++
title = "Description Templates"
description = "Using Gitlab's description templates"
[extra]
gitlab_issue = 65
+++

_**Description templates**_ are a feature on [Gitlab.com](https://gitlab.com) used to manage _issues_ and _merge requests_ - two other Gitlab features used to help guide conversation around a project. By default when someone goes to create a new issue or merge request in a repository, they're shown a form asking for a title and description, as well as other detials such as assignee, labels, and due date.

Description templates provide content that will be pre-populated into the description field of the form. Since the description allows for [Markdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markdown), that means the templates are Markdown as well, and in addition, can make use of Gitlab's [quick actions](https://docs.gitlab.com/ee/user/project/quick_actions.html).

Description templates are stored inside the repository:
```
/
│
└─ .gitlab/
          │
          └─ issue_templates/
          │                 │
          │                 └─ bug.md
          │                 │
          │                 └─ suggestion.md
          │
          └─ merge_request_templates/
```

Since these forms may be filled out by folk who have no association with the project, or no knowledge of Markdown, it's important that each template:
- be short.
- give enough explanation on its own.
- avoid unnecessary tech & formatting gibberish.

With that in mind, here's the basis for my `bug.md` description template:
```markdown
<!-- Label the scope of the bug you are reporting
Available labels are ~severe, ~interface -->
/label
<!-- If you're unsure, leave this blank. -->
<!-- Next, describe in your own words what went wrong: what were
     you doing, what happened, and what did you expect to happen? -->



<!-- Please ignore everything below this line. -->
/label ~bug
/assign @emsenn
```

Breaking down the template:
- Anything inside `<!-- -->` is a comment, and so won't show up when looking at the issue on Gitlab.com.
- The line `/label` starts a quick-action. If someone puts a valid label there, it will be assigned when the issue is created.
- The blank spot in the middle is where they write what happened. I've found it's usually better to leave this question simple than try and get a bunch of information at once. The one thing I do change is sometimes asking them to upload specific logs, depending on the project.
- The last two lines are more quickactions, to assign the `bug` label and assign the issue to me, simple enough.

Depending on the scope of the repository, it is usually useful to have several types of description templates.

For Issues, I've found it useful to have the following description templates:
- `bug.md`, for intaking bug reports
- `suggestions.md` for intaking general suggestions.

For merge requests, I've found these useful:
- _Actually haven't made any merge request templates yet._

As a final note, it may be useful to set up default issue and merge request templates
within your repository, to either be a generalized template based on the one above, or
instructions to select a template from the dropdown.
****** Gitlab Labels
+++
title = "Gitlab Labels"
description = "emsenn's approach"
+++

_Labels_ are a feature on [Gitlab.com](https://gitlab.com) used for categorizing _Issues_, which is a term developers use for "bug reports, feature suggestions, and so on." In many development flows, including my own, [every action starts as an Issue](https://about.gitlab.com/2016/03/03/start-with-an-issue/), so having them well-organized is important.

I organize my Gitlab labels into several categories, marked by color. Here is a list of categories I use, and several example labels for each:


- <span style="color:#A8D695;">**Language/platforms.**</span> These labels give some idea as to the skills needed to work on the issue. Some examples might be:
  - `html`
  - `angular`
  - `fiction-writing`
- <span style="color:#D9534F;">**Problems.**</span> These are for labeling issues that are highlight problems:
  - `bug`
  - `security`
  - `critical`
- <span style="color:#F0AD4E;">**Scope.**</span> What sort of change does this bring to the repository?
  - `refactor`
  - `new-feature`
  - `enhancement`
- <span style="color:#FFECDB;">**Gruntwork.**</span> Is this something that is easy, but just... tedious?
  - `technical-debt`
- <span style="color:#69D100;">**Input wanted.**</span> These are issues where contributions are actively wanted. Not necessarily code, maybe just conversation:
  - `discussion`
  - `question`
  - `help-wanted`
- <span style="color:#7F8C8D;">**Invalid.**</span> These are for tagging issues that for one reason another aren't really valid:
  - `duplicate`
  - `invalid`

There's another category of tag I use for small repositories that are just me, that don't warrant a calendar-based milestone system:
- <span style="color:#428BCA;">**Timeframe.**</span> To be assigned to tasks that are ready to be worked on, to demonstrate whether I plan to get to them _sooner_ or _later_:
  - `soon`
  - `later`

In addition to manually labeling Issues, I also use [description templates](../description-templates/) to quickly assign labels to new Issues.

A repository's Issue page defaults to sorting them by _priority_, which is determined by whether their labels are _prioritized_. Within the repository's settings on Gitlab.com, I mark every **problem** tag as priority, as well as the **soon** tag. The more prioritized tag an issue has associated with it, the higher up the list it is. That's why I usually have multiple **problem** tags, even on smaller repos - so I can throw more on to indicate that this `bug` is `critical` and `really-bad`.

This section /(is in early development and)/ contains procedures for training a member of
the staff on the use of emacs*** Emacs and Org-mode Training This section /(is in early
development and)/ contains training on how to use two pieces of software: *Emacs* and
*Org-mode*.

*/Emacs/* is the text editor that /Gill and Sennhauser/ uses, and */Org-mode/* is the
formatting that is applied to our source documents.  /(See the |TK| for specific
instructions on its use.)/

It is a requirement that *all* /Gill and Sennhauser/ staff be familiar with Emacs and
Org-mode, as it is our primary method of recording information.
****** Emacs and Org-mode Training, Module 1: Core Concepts
This module /(is in early development and)/ teaches you the basics of the Emacs text
editor and Org-mode formatting language.  /Gill and Sennhauser/ uses the two to conduct
most of its writing and recordkeeping, and as a part of their document publishing toolkit.

If you haven't yet, follow the Emacs and Org-mode setup to ensure your computer system is
properly prepared to run Emacs and Org-mode.

By the end of this module, you should feel comfortable:
- understanding the concepts behind the /menu bar/, /frame/, /window/, /buffer/, /status bar/, and
  /mini-buffer/.
- understanding the concepts behind /commands/, /hotkeys/, /major/ and /minor mode/, and
  moving around and changing the buffer.

******* Emacs and Org-mode Training, Module 1, Lesson 1:Anatomy of Emacs Display
In this lesson, you'll learn what you're looking at when you open Emacs.

When you open Emacs, a program will open that has several components.  From top to bottom,
those components are:
- The */menu bar/*, which is a mouse-interactable hierarchy of Emacs commands.
- The */frame/*, which is where most of Emacs is.  This is the entire space from the menu
  bar to the bottom of the program.  The frame can display multiple /windows/:
  - */Windows/* are where Emacs /buffers/ are shown, along with their /status bar/ and
    /mini-buffer/.
    - A */buffer/*, shows text, usually the contents of a file.  (Messages from Emacs,
      menus, agendas, and such are also buffers.)
    - The */status bar/* shows information about the file: if it's been changed since last
      saved, what /mode/ it's in, and other stuff.
    - The */mini-buffer/* shows you feedback on /commands/ you've entered and is where
      you'll type more information if the command needs it.

Assuming you have completed the Emacs setup procedure, your frame should be showing a
single window, displaying the ~*scratch*~ buffer.  This is a special buffer that's created
whenever you start Emacs.  It isn't associated with any file on your computer, but after
you've written in it, you can /write/ it to a file, or move the text to another buffer
(that is a file.)
******* Emacs and Org-Mode Training, Module One, Lesson Two: Interacting with Emacs
In this lesson, you'll learn how to interact with Emacs.  Start from where you left off in
Lesson One: just after opening Emacs.  /(If you already have it open, save what you were
working on and close it, then re-open it.)/

Assuming you've set up emacs, you should be looking at the ~*scratch*~ buffer, which
starts with a few lines explaining what you're looking at.  Within that buffer, you should
see a blinking blinking black cursor, in the upper left of the buffer.  This is the
*/point/*.  It shows where what you're typing (including /hotkeys/) will go.

Go ahead and type a few random letters. Avoid using any special keys like ~Ctrl~ or
~Alt~.  You'll see that they get inserted where the /point/ is.  Use your keyboard's arrow
keys to move the point around the buffer.  You may also use your mouse, but /using your
mouse is discouraged/.

Delete the letters you've entered before continuing.

The main way of interacting with Emacs is with */commands/*: text-based instructions about
what the program should do.  Here are some example commands:
- ~find-file~ prompts for a file name and then loads that file into a buffer with the same
  name.
- ~write-named-file~ prompts for a file name and then saves the buffer to that
  location. /(Save as)/
- ~org-insert-heading-respect-content~ places a new /heading/ at the end of the current
  /subtree/ (section).

These commands can be entered directory, or by using hotkeys.  Either way, you'll need to
understand the /syntax/ in which these commands and hotkeys are written, in /Gill and
Sennhauser/'s (and Emac's) documentation.  Here are some examples of this syntax:
- ~M^x find-file RET~ which says "Hold your ~Alt~ key, press the ~x~ key, release the
  ~Alt~ key, type ~find-file~, and press the ~Enter~ key," which runs the find-file
  command.
- ~C^x C^w~ which says "Hold your ~Ctrl~ key, press the ~x~ key, release the ~Ctrl~ key,
  hold ~Ctrl~ again, and press the ~w~ key," which runs the ~write-named-file~
  command.
- ~C-RET~ which says "Hold the ~Ctrl~ key and press ~Enter~," which runs the
  ~org-insert-heading-respect-content~ command.

Here is a legend explaining the syntax:
| Key/Concept       | How it's written       |
| ~Control~         | ~C~                    |
| ~Alt~             | ~M~                    |
| ~Shift~           | ~S~                    |
| ~Enter~           | ~RET~                  |
| ~Tab~             | ~TAB~                  |
| ~^~ or ~-~        | Hold the preceding key |
| Release held keys | ~(space)~              |

Now that you understand how to interact with Emacs, let's go over some basic operations:

***** DRAFTING Procedures for Online Communication              :@procedures:
    :PROPERTIES:
    :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedures-for-online-communication
    :END:
This {{{section}}} contains the procedures under which I conduct my */online
communication/*: sharing information with other people or organizations through the
Internet.

These procedures have been in implicit development my whole life, but I only started
explicitly considering how I want to conduct my online communication in early 2018.

The lists below demonstrate how I categorize my online communication, each linking to the
document for the relevant procedure:
- */[Direct](/procedure-for-direct-online-communication)/*
- */[Public](/procedures-for-public-online-communication)/*
  - *[Post](/procedures-for-posting-online)*
  - *[Publication](/procedure-for-publishing-online)*
****** DRAFTING Procedure for Direct Online Communication
     :PROPERTIES:
     :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedure-for-direct-online-communication
     :END:
Most of my direct online communication happens through an ever-changing medley of instant
messaging platforms defined more by contemporary trends than my opinion.

That is to say, I use whatever messaging applications my friends are using, and try not to
be picky about the procedure.
****** DRAFTING Procedures for Public Online Communication
     :PROPERTIES:
     :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedures-for-public-online-communication
     :END:
My public online communication is divided into two broad categories:
- */Posting/*, which is making a temporary but public post onto a platform.  This usually
  means /microblogging/.
- */Publishing/*, which is more formal: creating a document and releasing it through a
  platform.  This usually means posting to my personal website and writing an email to a
  /mailing list/.
******* DRAFTING Procedures for Posting Online
      :PROPERTIES:
      :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedures-for-posting-online
      :END:
When I want to */post/* something online, I want to send a quick message, usually to an
audience of /followers/, but also usually in a way that anyone could see it.  (After all,
I can't be sure that by sharing with my followers, I am not unknowingly be syndicated
publicly.)  */Followers/* are people who have subscribed to the relevant account or
stream.

There are a few things that inspire me to post:
- Reading something that I'd like to share my thoughts on.
- Doing work on an interesting project or piece of writing that I'd like to talk about.
******** Microblogging
The main way I post things is by */microblogging/*: short temporary posts that are
syndicated to followers' individual chronological feeds.  Specifically, I use an account
in the Fediverse to create posts:
[emsenn@mastodon.social](https;//mastodon.social/@emsenn).  (You can also view an [RSS
feed of my profile](https://mastodon.social/@emsenn.rss).

These are a few assumptions I make when microblogging:
- Since my audience chooses to see my posts, I assume they have an interest in the topics
  I'm posting about.
- Anyone might take anything I've said and use it out-of-context to my detriment.
- Anyone might find a way to say something to me about what I've posted.  If they have an
  account in the Fediverse, this is easy, but people find a way to make themselves heard
  if they want.

I do not think my microblog should be permanent, since I can't reasonably moderate the
posts that will show up in response to mine.  In that spirit, I have taken the rather
drastic step of deleting my posts that are older than 48 hours, unless I have "favorited"
it myself.  To accomplish this, I use [@codl](https://www.codl.fr/)'s
[Forget](https://forget.codl.fr/about/).
******** Into Data Silos
I recognize that a lot of potential and current personal and professional contacts don't
have accounts in the Fediverse, don't use RSS, and won't manually check my Mastodon
profile.

I maintain accounts on [Facebook](https://facebook.com/emsenn) and
[LinkedIn](https://linkedin.com/in/emsenn), though I have severe ethical criticisms of
both.  Take it as evidence that my desire to be cooperative outweighs my desire to boycott
monopolistic providers.

I don't post into these data silos as often as I post in the Fediverse, and tend to limit
my posts there to what would be of interest to a professional audience.
******* DRAFTING Procedure for Publishing Online
      :PROPERTIES:
      :EXPORT_FILE_NAME: procedure-for-publishing-online
      :END:
/Publishing/ something online is the most complicated way of communicating online, but also,
I think, the most useful. */Publishing/* here means sharing in a way that it becomes a
"permanent" part of the Internet.

I explain the specifics of how I write in my [Manual of Writing](/manual-of-writing), the
specifics of how I edit in my [Manual of Editing](/manual-of-editing), and the specifics
of how I create a document in my [Manual of Publishing](/manual-of-publishing).  (All of
which are in early development too.)

This procedure is about how I share that published document.

The first thing I do is add it to my website, [emsenn.net](https://emsenn.net), which is a
repository of all my published documents (and drafts).

Then, I write a post about it to the Fediverse, as described in my [procedures for posting
online](/procedures-for-posting-online).

Finally, I send the document to the relevant list of email contacts I maintain who have
expressed interest in its topic.

***** DRAFTING Planetside 2 Manual
+++
title = "Personal Planetside 2 Guide"
description = "This is for me and my buddy Tom, but I'm pleased if you find a use for it."
+++

### Checklists
#### Starting the Game
- Open your map with `M`.
- Cycle through every menu on the bottom row, waiting until they load. _(This helps them load faster when you need them.)_

### Radio Behavior
#### Transmitting
When you have something to say, and are on a channel with > 2 people, open the channel & say their username (or approximation), followed by `This is...` and your own name, followed by, `over`:
> Iguana this is Toaster, over.

**If there's radio chatter and you want everyone to shut up, lead your message with a firm `Break, break!`**

After the person you're addressing has acknowledged receipt of your introduction, you may give your next message.

Other rules:
- Always say numbers by the digit. 1914 is `one-niner-one-four`
- Spell things out with the NATO phonetic alphabet
- If you know the thing bears repeating, preface your reptition with `say again`
- Give directions by compass direction if everyone is on foot, or by "bearing & yankee" if in vehicle, where bearing is angle from facing stern along a horizontal axis, and yankee is height (y-axis), divided into chunks of twelve. (Like a clock). This is the only time you get to say `ten`, `eleven`, `twelve`, instead of `one-zero`, `one-one`, `one-two`.

#### Receiving
When you hear a message addressed to you (or your squad, if you're squad leader), give acknowledgement of receipt with one of the following, in order of preference
- `Romeo`
- `Received`
- `Acknowledged`
- `Copy`

After you've received each message, you again acknowledge receipt. If the message is instructions, you may respond with `wilco` for "will comply". If the message is unclear, say `repeat`.

#### Example conversation
_(Iguana is piloting an airship while Toaster is gunning it)_
> Iguana this is Toaster, over.
>
> Toaster, Iguana romeo.
>
> Iguana, bring us to bearing two-four-zero.
>
> Toaster, wilco.
>
> Iguana, any plans for the weekend af-
>
> Break, break! Hostile at bearing seven yankee ten.
>
> Toaster, iguana, repeat.
>
> Hostile at bearing seven yankee ten, repeat seven yankee ten.

***** Package Management

#+name: package-management
#+begin_src elisp
  (defvar straight-install-script-path
    "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/raxod502/straight.el/develop/install.el")
  
  (defvar bootstrap-version)
  (let ((bootstrap-file
	 (expand-file-name "straight/repos/straight.el/bootstrap.el" user-emacs-directory))
	(bootstrap-version 5))
    (unless (file-exists-p bootstrap-file)
      (with-current-buffer
      (url-retrieve-synchronously straight-install-script-path
       'silent 'inhibit-cookies)
	(goto-char (point-max))
	(eval-print-last-sexp)))
    (load bootstrap-file nil 'nomessage))
  
  (straight-use-package 'use-package)
#+end_src

******* Org Export HTML Sitemap
#+name: org-export-html-sitemap
#+begin_src elisp
      ;; from https://www.john2x.com/blog/blogging-with-orgmode.html
      (defun emacsen-org-publish-format-sitemap (title sitemap)
	(let ((posts (cdr sitemap)))
	  (concat
	   (format "#+title: %s\n" title)
	   "#+author: emsenn\n"
	   "#+email: emsenn@emsenn.net\n"
	   "#+options: timestamp:nil date:nil\n"
	   (format "#+date: %s\n" (format-time-string "[%Y-%m-%d %a %H:%M:%S]" (current-time)))
	   "#+roam_tags: meta\n"
	   (org-list-to-org (cons (car sitemap) posts)))))
#+end_src
******* Org Export HTML RSS
#+name: org-export-html-rss
#+begin_src elisp
	(defun emacsen-org-rss-publish-to-rss (plist filename pub-dir)
	  "Publish RSS with PLIST, only when FILENAME is 'rss.org'. PUB-DIR is when the output will be placed."
	  (if (equal "rss.org" (file-name-nondirectory filename))
	      (org-rss-publish-to-rss plist filename pub-dir)))
  
	(defun emacsen-format-rss-feed (title list)
	  "Generate RSS feed, as a string. TITLE is the title of the RSS feed.  LIST is an internal representation for the files to include, as returned by `org-list-to-lisp'.  PROJECT is the current project."
	  (concat "#+TITLE: " title "\n\n"
		  (org-list-to-subtree list 1 '(:icount "" :istart ""))))
	(defun emacsen-format-rss-feed-entry (entry style project)
	  "Format ENTRY for the RSS feed. ENTRY is a file name.  STYLE is either 'list' or 'tree'. PROJECT is the current project."
	  (cond ((not (directory-name-p entry))
		 (let* ((file (org-publish--expand-file-name entry project))
			(title (org-publish-find-title entry project))
			(date (format-time-string "%Y-%m-%d" (org-publish-find-date entry project)))
			(link (concat (file-name-sans-extension entry) ".html")))
		   (with-temp-buffer
		     (insert (format "* [[file:%s][%s]]\n" file title))
		     (org-set-property "RSS_PERMALINK" link)
		     (org-set-property "PUBDATE" date)
		     (insert-file-contents file)
		     (buffer-string))))
		((eq style 'tree)
		 ;; Return only last subdir.
		 (file-name-nondirectory (directory-file-name entry)))
		(t entry)))    
#+end_src

********* emsenn.net Preamble Copy
#+name: emsenn-net-preamble-copy
#+begin_src elisp
  "<p>This Website was cultivated as a public asset with <a href=\"https://ko-fi.com/emsenn\">support</a> from people like you. <strong><em>Thank you!</em></strong></p>
  <details>
    <summary>Click this sentence for information about my Website.</summary>
    <p>Click each sentence below for more info:</p>
      <ul>
	<li><details>
	  <summary>This website was created by Emacs and Org-mode</summary>
	  <p>See <a href=\"https://emsenn.net/per/emacsen.html\">my Emacs configuration</a> to learn how.</p>
	</details></li>
	<li><details>
	  <summary>It uses style to add information to the pages.</summary>
	  <ul>
	    <li><details>
	    <summary>Click this sentence to see how links are styled:</summary>
	    <table>
	      <thead><tr>
		<th scope=\"col\">Color</th>
		<th scope=\"col\">Link type</th>
	      </tr></thead>
	      <tbody>
		<tr><td class=\"link\">Sample</td>
		  <td>Unvisited local resource</td></tr>
		<tr><td class=\"visited-link\">Sample</td>
		  <td>Visited local resource</td></tr>
		<tr><td class=\"external-link\">Sample</td>
		  <td>Unvisited external resource</td></tr>
		<tr><td class=\"visited-external-link\">Sample</td>
		  <td>Visited external resource</td></tr>
	      </tbody>
	    </table>
	  </details></li>
	</ul>
      </details></li>
    </ul>
  </details>
  "
#+end_src

******** emsenn.net CSS
#+name: emsenn-net-css
#+begin_src elisp
      (setq emsenn-net-org-html-head
	    (concat "<style>
	   @charset \"UTF-8\";
	   
	   :root {	
       --body-font: sans-serif;
	       --header-font: sans-serif;
	       --code-font: monospace;
	       --primary-color: #ffe;
	       --primary-dim-color: #eed;
	       --secondary-color: #312;
	       --secondary-dim-color: #756;
	       --link-color: #880;
	       --link-hover-color: #aa2;
	       --link-visited-color: #934;
	       --link-visited-hover-color: #b56;
	       --external-link-color: #008;
	       --external-link-hover-color: #60b;
	       --external-link-visited-color: #172;
	       --external-link-visited-hover-color: #394;
	   }
	   @media(prefers-color-scheme:dark){
	       :root {
		  --body-font: sans-serif;
		  --header-font: sans-serif;
		  --code-font: monospace;
		  --primary-color: #756;
		  --primary-dim-color: #312;
		  --secondary-color: #eed;
		  --secondary-dim-color: #ffe;
		  --link-color: #880;
		  --link-hover-color: #aa2;
		  --link-visited-color: #934;
		  --link-visited-hover-color: #b56;
	       }}
	   body {
	       position: relative;
	       min-height: 100%;
	       background-color: var(--primary-color);
	       color: var(--secondary-color);
	       margin: 0 auto;
	       font-family: sans-serif;
	   }
	   main, #content {
	       background-color: var(--primary-dim-color);
	       border-left: 1.5em dotted var(--primary-color);
	       padding: 1em;
	   }
	   header {
	       border-bottom: 0.8em dotted var(--secondary-dim-color);
	   }
	   h1 { font-size: 2em; margin-bottom: 0; }
	   h2 {
	       border-bottom: 0.3em dotted var(--secondary-dim-color);
	   }
	   h2, h3, h4, h5, h6, p, table, ul, ol, .org-src-container {
	       width: 60%;
	   }
	   aside {
	       float: right;
	       width: 35%;
	   }
	   aside p { width: 100%; }
	   @media screen and (max-width:760px) {
	       h2, h3, h4, h5, h6, p, table, ul, ol, .org-src-container {
		  width: 90%;
	       }
	       aside {
		  float: inherit;
		  width: 90%;
		  border-top: 0.2em dotted var(--secondary-dim-color);
		  border-bottom: 0.2em dotted VAR(--secondary-dim-color);
		  font-size: 0.8em;
		    }
	   }
	   
	   footer, #postamble, #preamble {
	       padding: 0.5em;
	   }
	   table {
	       border-collapse: collapse;
	       border-spacing: 0;
	       empty-cells: show;
	       border: 0.1em solid #444;
	   }
	   thead { font-family: sans-serif; }
	   th, td {
	       padding: 0 0.3em;
	       border: 0.01em solid #444;
	   }
	   img {
	       max-width: 80vw;
	       vertical-align: middle;
	   }
	   pre, .src {
	       padding: 0.5em;
	       border: 0.1em solid #444;
	       white-space: pre-wrap;
	       overflow-x: scroll;
	       text-overflow: clip;
	       scrollbar-width: none;
	   }
	   pre::-webkit-scrollbar, .src::-webkit-scrollbar {
	       display: none;
	   }
	   
	   
	   dt {
	       font-weight: normal;
	       display: inline-block;
	       border-bottom: 0.1rem dotted #444;
	   }
	   #text-index dt { border-bottom: none; }
	   h2, h3, h4, h5, h6, h7, h8, h9, h10 {
	       display: block;
	       font-family: sans-serif;
	       margin: 0.2em auto 0.5em 0;
	   }
	   a, .link {
	       text-decoration: none;
	       color: #880;
	       display: inline;
	       position: relative;
	       border-bottom: 0.1rem dotted;
	       line-height: 1.2;
	       transition: border 0.3s;
	   }
	   a:hover {
	       color: #aa2;
	       outline-style: none;
	       border-bottom: 0.1rem solid;
	   }
	   a:visited, .visited-link { color: #934; }
	   a:visited:hover { color: #b56; }
	   a:focus {
	       outline-style: none;
	       border-bottom: 0.1rem solid;
	   }
	   ::selection { background-color: #777; color: #eee; }
	   a::selection { background-color: #ccc; }
	   .org-todo {
	       background-color: #ddd;
	       color: #333;
	       font-size: .8rem;
	       float: right;
	   }
	   .org-src-container + .example {
	       margin-left: 8em;
	   }
	   a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]):after, a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]):visited:after, .external-link:after, .visited-external-link:after { 
	       content: \"^\";
	       font-size: 0.5em;
	       vertical-align: super;
	   }
	   a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]), .external-link {
	       color: var(--external-link-color);
	   }
	   a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]):hover{ 
	       color: var(--external-link-hover-color);
	   }
	   a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]):visited, .visited-external-link { 
	       color: var(--external-link-visited-color);
	   }
	   a[href^=\"https://\"]:not([href*=\"emsenn.net\"]):visited:hover{ 
	       color: var(--external-link-visited-hover-color);
	   }
	   blockquote {
	       border: 0.1em dashed var(--secondary-dim-color);
	       padding-left: 0.2em;
	   }
	   label.org-src-name {
	       margin: 0 0 0 3em;
	       font-family: sans-serif;
	       font-size: 1rem;
	       line-height: 0;
	   }
	   .skip-to-content {
	       opacity: 0;
	       position: absolute;
	       left: 0.2em;
	   }
	   .skip-to-content:focus{
	       opacity: 1;
	       background-color: var(--secondary-dim-color);
	       color: var(--primary-color);
	   }
	   ul { max-width: 85%; }
    .footpara { display: inline; font-size: 0.8rem; }
  </style>"))
#+end_src

** Lists
*** Pre-Purchase Questions

These questions are designed to help me discern some information about something I might purchase that isn't available from its market specification, like, "How likely is it I can purchase this when intercontinental shipping isn't affordable?"
- Where did it come from?
  - How was it shipped here?
    - Air freight? Fuck that.
    - Transocean? Not in the future.
